Step 5.5 and Phase 3.5 keep the reminder but hand delivery to Step 0, because their own "before the first session" timing was unsatisfiable from inside a step that only runs once a session is already under way.
- **The marker-contract row names both directories**, so the agent-side contract and the operator-side README agree on what to mention.
- **Step 5.5 and Phase 3.5 both explain the mechanism rather than asserting it** — untracked artifacts plus an `after_doing` section that stages everything equals a commit — so an operator can see why the line is needed and judge whether their own hook has that shape.
- **It stays operator guidance.** The agent tells the operator to add the entry, exactly as it does for `.stride/`, and **never edits their `.gitignore` itself**; an unrequested repository mutation is not the fix for this.
- **`.exploratory/` is the default, not the only location.** `/explore`, `/pair` and `/harden` each take an `--output` flag that puts artifacts wherever the operator names, and the entry protects none of that — so both skills and the README say an operator who redirects artifacts needs that path gitignored too. The artifact carries the same transcribed output either way.
- **Nothing requires the directory to exist.** A `.gitignore` entry for a path that never appears is inert, and on the sanctioned dispatch path nothing writes there at all — nothing in the `explorer` agent's contract asks it to write a session file. The entry is for the sessions an operator runs themselves, which is where artifacts actually come from.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible, and additive only. Prompt text and docs — no completion field, enum, hook, or schema change, and no change to any trigger condition or to the graceful-skip contract. Nothing is required of a project that has no `.exploratory/` directory, and a project whose `.gitignore` already covers it needs no action. The plugin's own behaviour is unchanged: it did not commit before this release and does not now — the staging it warns about belongs to the project's own `## after_doing` commands.

### Scope

The two orchestrator skills and the docs. No `.gitignore` in this repository or any other is modified by this change; that is the operator's call, which is the point.

## [1.51.0] - 2026-07-30

### Changed — the completion summary now names who is harmed, and cites the session artifact when one exists (W1976)

A reviewer reading `completion_notes` got a paragraph that had already lost most of the session. It carried each bug's severity but never who the bug lands on, and it never pointed at the fuller record even when one had been written. Both are fixed in the two carriers the section already uses, at no schema cost.

- **Stakeholder impact joins the summary.** Severity says how bad a failure is; it never says who it harms, and that is what drives triage. The guidance now asks for both. The impact is read from the installed explorer contract's own field where it has one — **and that is version-dependent**, exactly as the budget unit was in 1.50.0: an older contract emits no impact field, in which case the agent states who is harmed from what the finding shows, or says plainly that the session did not establish it. It is restated in the agent's own words, never pasted, and never invented to fill the gap.
- **The artifact path is cited when one exists — and the guidance is honest that usually none does.** The only surface Step 5.5 may dispatch, the `explorer` agent, **is not asked to write one**: nothing in its contract instructs it to write a session file and no sanctioned path asks it to. (Stated that narrowly on purpose — it holds no `Write`, but its `Bash` is unrestricted, so neither its tool list nor its output contract, which governs what it returns rather than what it does on the way, would establish a stronger claim.) So the automated path produces no artifact at all, and the prose summary is not a degraded fallback there but the normal and complete record. A path is cited only when the agent actually knows of an artifact a human separately produced that belongs to this task's record — never by hunting for a file, and never inferred from a default path that may hold a different session's output.
- **Path, never contents.** Citing is a pointer, not an upload. The artifact may hold raw session output that was never redacted, and the completion payload leaves the machine — so the guidance forbids reading it in or attaching it, prefers a repository-relative path since an absolute one discloses username and home directory for no benefit, and says to describe an out-of-repository artifact in general terms rather than pasting its full path.
- **The redaction rule is restated for both — and hardened, after an exploratory session found three ways it leaked.** Impact text comes from observed application behaviour and can carry customer identifiers or internal hostnames; a path can disclose environment layout. Both are now named explicitly, and three further gaps the session surfaced are closed:
  - **A length bound is not a credential control.** The unrecognized-severity rule quoted "at most the first 40 characters" of a raw value as its mitigation — but real secrets are shorter than that, so a ~32-character live-mode key survived the truncation whole while the instruction looked like it had been obeyed. The bound is replaced by a **value-class test**: anything credential-shaped is not quoted at all, truncated or otherwise, and is replaced by a sentinel naming what was seen. The backtick fencing remains, but it is now stated to address injection only — legibility as a secret is a separate problem it never solved.
  - **`completion_summary` is in the sink list.** Both orchestrators name three persisted sinks and delegate the method here, where only two were named — and the omitted one is the field that is required, persisted, and actually rendered on the Review queue. The scope of the obligation was narrowing as it crossed documents, in the wrong direction.
  - **Restating is not redacting.** "In your own words" changes phrasing, and identifiers are not phrasing: a faithful paraphrase carries an account name, a customer email and a hostname through untouched. The two are now stated as separate obligations, with what redaction actually looks like — generalise the referent, keep what a reader needs to triage, drop what identifies — and the same name-the-finding-rather-than-quote-it convention the workflow already uses for a credential-bearing matrix row.
  - The field list is now explicitly open rather than a five-of-ten enumeration that a reader could take as a checklist, and it names the hazard-dense fields the earlier list omitted — including the one the impact line itself draws from. A redaction warning is also front-loaded at the point composition begins, since the rule previously sat last of nine paragraphs, after the text it governs already existed, with no gate downstream.
- **`completion_summary` gains a mirror on this path too.** Where no reviewer ran, `completion_notes` is the only carrier — and it is persisted only from D188 onward, so a session that surfaced real bugs could reach nobody on an older server. One line now mirrors into `completion_summary`, matching what the refusal, review-reversal and escalation records in this workflow already require. The section's claim that `completion_notes` is "always available" is corrected to say available *to write to*, which is what was true.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt text only — **no new completion field and no seventh `workflow_steps` name**; the impact and the artifact path land in `completion_notes` and the `reviewer_result.testing_strategy` note, the two carriers the section already used, and the `completion_summary` line is a durability mirror into an **existing required field** rather than a third independent record — so the two-carrier structure is extended, not restructured, and no schema changes. The plugin-not-used fallback is untouched: with the plugin absent, no `manual_tests`, or a non-Claude-Code environment, the completion records nothing extra, exactly as before. A session that wrote no artifact simply carries no path, which is the ordinary case rather than an error state.

### Scope

The completion skill and the docs. The `stride-exploratory-testing` plugin is untouched. Session artifacts are not yet gitignored anywhere in this repo; that is deliberately left to its own task rather than folded in here.

An exploratory session also surfaced three defects in `stride-workflow` Step 5.5 and its Phase 3.5 mirror — a blocked session having no coverage disposition where the identical zero-coverage ending has one, an instruction to report an unreachable app "as a finding" that the explorer contract cannot emit, and a capture sentence naming session-sheet fields an older contract lacks. Those lines belong to the previous release rather than this change, so following the introduced-versus-discovered policy this goal introduced, they were filed as **D205** rather than folded in.

## [1.50.0] - 2026-07-30

### Changed — the Step 5.5 dispatch now carries an explicit session budget and a named environment-context list (W1975)

The dispatch handed the explorer "the running-app environment context" and left the rest to inference. Two things were wrong with that. The agent's contract expects a **session budget**, and with none supplied it silently fell back to its own default — so an autonomous workflow was dispatching unbounded sessions against live applications, which is both a runaway risk and a larger blast radius than a bounded one. And the environment context was named but never enumerated, so the caller could omit something the session then had no way to obtain, since the explorer is documented as never asking the user a question.

- **The budget is stated in whatever unit the installed agent contract declares** — read it from that contract, since the two repositories release independently (see the version-skew bullet below). In the current contract that unit is **probes**: default **12**, usable band **8–20**, with a **tool-call ceiling** defaulting to **5× the probe budget** (60 at the default) as the backstop for a session that spins rather than probes, whichever ceiling it reaches first ending the session. Both skills now say the budget is **the caller's to set** and give guidance for choosing rather than a hard-coded number: the low end of the band for a narrow charter or a task with many `manual_tests` to get through, the high end for a broad one worth a deep look, the default absent a reason to move off it.
- **The version-aware handling covers the endings too, not just the unit.** An older contract reports no stop reason at all — only `completed` / `stopped_early` / `blocked` — and `stopped_early` is ambiguous between partial coverage and a session that never got going, which are opposite dispositions. The skills give the mapping and say to resolve the ambiguity from the session sheet's own account of coverage, taking the conservative reading when it shows little. The tool-call-ceiling disposition is likewise judged on probes actually run rather than on the ceiling alone, since a ceiling hit after meaningful probing is partial coverage rather than a non-session.
- **The unit is established from the installed contract, not from this page.** An exploratory session run against a draft of this change found the hazard live: the `explorer` version installed here takes a **wall-clock time box** (~90 minutes), while the probe budget belongs to a newer version present only as a working copy. A caller following a hard-coded probe count would have stated a budget in a unit the dispatched agent did not recognise, withheld the input that agent marks required, and triggered its ~90-minute default — producing exactly the unmeasured duration the instruction exists to prevent. Both skills now say to read the unit from the contract actually installed, carry the current numbers as a dated reference, and name the older wall-clock unit so a caller meeting it gives it the box it asks for. The rule is the constant; the unit is not.
- **Never a wall-clock time box *to a contract that asks for probes*.** That caution is now scoped rather than absolute, for the same reason. Step 5.5's description of the session was corrected from "time-boxed" to "budgeted" to match the vocabulary the current agent uses.
- **Every dispatch input now has a stated source.** Naming inputs without saying where they come from left four of them unsourced, and the authorization affirmative was the sharpest: it is required, may not be supplied on the user's behalf, and the workflow forbids prompting between steps — so as first drafted the gate had no reachable satisfying path and would have made the step either permanently skipped or quietly rationalised. **Step 0 gained the collection** — one question, asked at the one point in the lifecycle where asking is legal, covering the authorized/non-production affirmative, how to reach the app, and where test accounts live; the answers carry forward to every dispatch. It is optional and never blocks: no plugin, a declined question, or anything short of an explicit affirmative simply records that and moves on, and Step 5.5 then skips with no failure. Inference from a `localhost` URL or from task text stays explicitly barred, task text being author-written and already distrusted here for safety-bearing decisions. App reach and seed-data pointers get sources too, with the distinction that failing to establish a URL is not the same condition as an unreachable app.
- **The call shape is stated.** The agent takes exactly two arguments — a charter and one free-text environment-context block — so the input list now says the other entries are contents of that block rather than separate named fields, which a bulleted list of bolded labels otherwise implies.
- **The quick references carry the two safety-bearing inputs.** The flowchart, the quick-reference tree and the capability table each named the sanctioned surface but dropped both the budget and the authorization gate, so a reader working from a summary — which is what summaries are for — would have dispatched unbounded and ungated.
- **Budget exhaustion never fails completion — but the three ways a session can end are not the same coverage claim.** A first draft equated all of them, and an exploratory session run against it showed why that is wrong: a **quiet charter** means the area was covered and is the only ending that supports "this manual test was performed"; a spent **probe budget** means partial coverage, valid findings, and a claim that must say so; a spent **tool-call ceiling** means the session was spinning rather than probing, and because setup and orientation consume tool calls without consuming probe budget, a setup-heavy charter can hit it having run **zero probes and produced nothing**. Equating that last case with a quiet charter would have recorded a session that did not happen as a manual test that did — strictly worse than not running the plugin, which at least leaves the test visibly owed. The ceiling case is therefore judged on probes actually run: at or near zero it is recorded as not performed and handed back as a human responsibility, while a ceiling hit after meaningful probing is partial coverage like a spent probe budget. Later endings a contract may report — *risk acceptable*, which reads as a quiet charter, and *blocked*, which is the unreachable-app path — are named too, with a catch-all for any not listed. None of these fails completion; only the honest coverage claim differs, and the Decision Summary gained six rows covering them.
- **A budget too small to fund one workable session is a reason not to dispatch at all.** Below the band's low end, or a charter whose setup alone would exhaust the ceiling, the skills now say to skip and note the manual tests as a human responsibility rather than run a token session that cannot reach the feature. The band is also stated to be **per dispatch** rather than a pool to divide, since many `manual_tests` need proportionally more total budget, not a thinner slice each.
- **"Follow-up charter" is no longer offered as a disposition.** It named no artifact, owner, or verb, and a charter is a transient dispatch input with no identifier and no life past the session — so discharging unexamined risk to one dropped it. Leftover risk is now named in `completion_notes` and **filed as a follow-up defect or task** with its ID in the record, on the same terms the discovered-Critical branch already uses.
- **The whole agent return is captured, including the session sheet.** The capture instruction previously named the summary and the bug list, omitting the sheet — the sole carrier of how the session ended and how many probes actually ran, which the exhaustion rule then depended on. Enumerating fields is also the failure mode this workflow already warns about for `reviewer_result`, so the instruction is now a passthrough. Both skills additionally say to record in `completion_notes` how the session ended and what it covered, not only what it found, since otherwise an exhausted session and a complete one produce identical records for the Review-queue human.
- **The introduced-Critical re-run must re-reach the defect.** The new budget rule made "re-run the affected charter" satisfiable by a re-run that stopped before ever reaching the fix, which would have read as confirmation that it held. The re-run now has to re-execute the finding's own minimal repro, and a re-run that stops on budget first has verified nothing.
- **The environment context is now enumerated**, matching the input set the explorer's own contract expects: how to reach the running app, the authorized non-production confirmation, which interaction tools are available this session, where test accounts or seed data live, and the budget.
- **Credentials are pointed at, never inlined.** The skills now say in as many words that the caller references where test accounts and seed data live and never pastes real credentials, tokens, or customer data into the dispatch — the plugin's own environment-context rule, restated on the Stride side because the dispatch prompt is an artifact like any other.
- **The authorized non-production confirmation is named as a safety gate, not a formality.** The agent treats an unauthorized or unclear target as out of bounds, and the orchestrator may not satisfy that gate on the user's behalf — so a dispatch without that affirmative in hand is not made at all, and the step is skipped and noted exactly as when the app is unreachable.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt text only — no completion field, enum, hook, or schema change, and no new `workflow_steps` name. Both trigger conditions and the graceful-skip contract are untouched: with the plugin absent, no `manual_tests`, or a non-Claude-Code environment the step is skipped with no failure, exactly as before. A dispatch that previously omitted the budget got the agent's default; it now gets an explicit value in the same unit, so behaviour is unchanged where the default was already right and bounded deliberately where it was not. The new "do not dispatch without the authorization affirmative" instruction narrows rather than widens what runs.

### Scope

The two orchestrator skills and the CHANGELOG. `README.md` also describes the session as "time-boxed" and is left alone — it is outside this task's stated files, and the correction belongs with whoever next edits that section.

## [1.49.0] - 2026-07-30

### Changed — the Step 5.5 dispatch is restricted to non-interactive surfaces, so `/pair` is never auto-dispatched (W1974)

Step 5.5 and Phase 3.5 said "dispatch the exploratory-testing plugin," which named a plugin rather than a surface. That was safe while the plugin's only session-runners were `/explore` and the `explorer` agent, but the plugin has since grown commands that stop and wait for a person — and the orchestrator explicitly does not prompt the user between steps. An interactive command dispatched into an unattended workflow does not fail; it stalls, silently, until the 60-minute claim expires.

- **The rule is a principle, not a list:** dispatch only a surface that runs to completion without a human, and judge whatever the plugin gains later by whether it can complete unattended rather than by whether it is enumerated here. If that cannot be established, it is not dispatched.
- **"Surface" spans commands, agents *and skills*, with two consequences an enumeration of commands would have missed.** A surface that merely **routes** to another surface can never be shown to complete unattended, because what it will hand the work to is unknown in advance — which bars the plugin's own front-door routing skill, whose job is to route a request shaped exactly like this step's to a sub-skill or slash command, `/pair` among them. That skill is also what the bare plugin name resolves to in the available-skills list, so the old "dispatch the exploratory-testing plugin" phrasing landed on precisely the surface that can reach the interactive commands. And a surface is disqualified by the prompts it **can** raise, not only those it always raises: a conditional prompt still stalls the run where it fires, the orchestrator does not control every input that decides it, and a prompt that exists as a **safety control** disqualifies outright, since satisfying such a gate on the user's behalf is never the orchestrator's call. `/recon` is the clean case for the safety-control clause, and `/explore` for the non-pre-emptible prompt — its question round asks which interaction tools the session has, which no argument can answer.
- **Sanctioned — exactly one surface:** the `stride-exploratory-testing:explorer` agent, one charter per dispatch. A subagent structurally cannot prompt mid-run, and this one is documented as never asking the user anything.
- **Not `/explore`, despite it being the plugin's headline command.** Applying the principle honestly ruled it out. Its opening question round is unconditional, and one of the four things it gathers is the session's available interaction tools — which the command's own text says it must ask for, because "a slash command cannot enumerate its own session's tool inventory." That question cannot be pre-answered by supplying arguments, so the round can never be left with nothing to ask, and an unattended dispatch stalls on it. An earlier draft of this change sanctioned `/explore` on the assumption that supplying the target, URL, auth and non-production confirmation would satisfy the round; reading the command established that it would not. `/explore` remains a fine thing for a human to run.
- **Never auto-dispatched, human-initiated only:** `/pair` above all — the plugin's designated human-at-the-keyboard surface, whose own description says the whole command is a conversation and whose allow-list deliberately withholds `Agent` and `WebFetch` so it cannot drive the app itself; dispatching it unattended waits forever on a human who was never invited. Also `/nightmare-headline` (a sustained interactive brainstorm), `/recon` (its human authorization confirmation is a safety control the orchestrator may not satisfy on the user's behalf).
- **The test is "requires a human," not "asks a human."** A surface that issues no prompt but waits on an out-of-band approval, review, or acknowledgement stalls the task exactly as a prompting one does, so every restatement now reads the broad way and the shorter phrasings are marked as shorthand for it rather than as a narrowing.
- **Which conditional prompts disqualify is now a stated test rather than an induction from examples.** A prompt you can pre-empt by supplying an input you control does not disqualify — a command that asks only when its target argument is missing is fine, so supply the target. A prompt fired by a condition you do not control does, since you cannot guarantee the firing run will not be yours. A prompt that is a **safety control** disqualifies outright regardless. Applying it honestly moved `/harden` **off** the never-list: both of its prompts are pre-emptible — a bug source you can pass positionally, and a framework you can pin with `--framework`, which the command's own text calls an operator override — so it belongs with `/charter` and `/debrief` as non-interactive but simply not a session runner. An earlier draft had it on the never-list for a rationale the command disproves; this is the second place in this entry where reading the plugin overturned a stated justification, the first being `/explore`.
- **How to establish the property is stated.** Read a surface's front matter and prompt body — `description`, `allowed-tools`, and the conditions under which its text says it asks anything — as data. That is reading, not running, and is explicitly distinguished from the barred practice of executing untrusted plugin content to probe for availability, which a cautious reader could otherwise have taken as forbidding the very inspection the principle requires.
- **The operative dispatch step no longer re-closes the list the principle opens.** It now says the `explorer` agent is the only surface qualifying *today*, and that a future one qualifies by satisfying the principle rather than by being enumerated — so a reader who jumps straight to the numbered procedure, as an executing agent does, gets the open rule rather than a closed pair.
- **The entries describe a separately-versioned repo, and now say so.** Every claim about what a surface asks or what its allow-list withholds was read at a point in time, and `stride-exploratory-testing` releases on its own cadence, so the skills instruct re-establishing a surface from its own text whenever that plugin's version changes. The subsection also gained the reciprocal keep-in-sync note that the neighbouring escalation policy already had.
- **Availability detection is not dispatch licence.** The detection list answers "is the plugin installed," and it names `/recon` and `/nightmare-headline` among the sibling commands it looks for — both on the never-dispatch list — as well as `/explore`. Both skills now say so in as many words, so the two lists cannot be confused for one another.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible, and strictly narrowing. Prompt text only — no completion field, enum, hook, or schema change. **No trigger condition moves and the graceful-skip contract is untouched**: the gate is still non-empty `manual_tests` plus plugin availability, and with the plugin absent, no `manual_tests`, or a non-Claude-Code environment the step is skipped with no failure exactly as before. Nothing that was **safely** dispatchable becomes undispatchable. The previous dispatch step named `/explore` and the `explorer` agent; the agent remains sanctioned and `/explore` is deliberately no longer sanctioned, because reading the command established that it cannot be driven unattended — so what was withdrawn was never safe to dispatch in the first place, and the agent covers the same case. The detection gate is untouched: this narrows what may be *run*, never what counts as *installed*. The change removes a stall condition rather than granting any capability.

### Scope

The two orchestrator skills and the docs. The `stride-exploratory-testing` plugin is untouched — this restricts what the Stride workflow may dispatch and asserts nothing about how those commands behave for a human running them deliberately.

## [1.48.0] - 2026-07-30

### Added — the exploratory rubric now maps onto the reviewer severity enum, and a Critical finding has a stated escalation policy (W1973)

Two optional integrations hang off the same phase with opposite consequences. The `stride-security-review` sub-step escalates fail-closed — a `partial`/`unmitigated` verdict forces `security_considerations` to `"failed"` and appends a Critical issue. The `stride-exploratory-testing` step recorded whatever the session found as advisory prose and stopped there. That difference was never decided; it fell out of severity being undefined. `stride-completing-tasks` asked for "any bugs surfaced (with severity)" without saying *which* severity vocabulary, and the exploratory plugin's four-level ladder (**Critical > High > Moderate > Minor**) and `reviewer_result.issues[].severity` (`critical` / `important` / `minor`) had no stated relationship. A Critical defect found while exercising the very code under review should not be silently advisory — but an unrelated pre-existing bug the session happened to surface must not block a task that did not cause it, or authors will simply stop enabling the integration.

- **`stride-completing-tasks`** gains a severity-mapping table: Critical → `critical`, High → `important`, **Moderate → `important`**, Minor → `minor`. The four-into-three collapse has to lose one boundary, and the entry records why it loses High/Moderate rather than Moderate/Minor: the reviewer enum's values are dispositions at the completion gate, not descriptions — `critical` and `important` both mean *fix before proceeding*, `minor` means *optional* — so the boundary to lose is the one whose two sides share a disposition. Filing a broken export beside a truncated label would be the deflation `bug-advocacy` warns costs as much credibility as inflation. An absent or unrecognized severity maps to `important` and is never dropped: `critical` is the only value that triggers the escalation, and escalating on a string that would not parse would let malformed or application-controlled text reach a blocking path. This **maps** the rubric; it does not redefine it, and a mapped value is never written back onto the explorer's `bugs[].severity`.
- **`stride-workflow` Step 5.5 and `stride-subagent-workflow` Phase 3.5** gain the escalation policy, in substance identical and cross-referenced as such in both directions. Only a mapped `critical` reaches it, and each Critical in a multi-finding session is tested separately. **Introduced** escalates **fail-closed in the same shape the security sub-step already uses**: `testing_strategy.status` → `"failed"`, a `category: "testing"` / `severity: "critical"` entry appended to `issues[]`, `issue_counts.critical` and `issues_found` incremented to match, and the defect fixed, the charter re-run, and the **reviewer re-run** before completing — the fresh review is what clears the escalation, which is why the remedy is a re-review rather than a hand-edit of the entry just appended. **Discovered** appends nothing and flips nothing: it is recorded in `completion_notes` at its exploratory severity and in one line of `completion_summary`, reflected in the `testing_strategy` note without changing its status, and filed as a follow-up defect.
- **The test is one decidable question, anchored to the agent's own artifacts: are the responsible lines among the lines this task added or modified?** The agent localizes the finding to its **fault site** — the lines that produce the wrong behaviour, not the call chain that reaches them — then compares against its own change set: every line changed relative to the task's base ref, **including staged, unstaged, and untracked-new files** (a bare `git diff` misses the last two, so a defect in a module the task just created would wrongly read as "not mine"), **minus the claim-time dirty baseline** — edits already in the working tree at claim time satisfy "changed relative to the base" but are not lines the task wrote, and `git blame` cannot tell them apart because pre-claim edits also read `Not Committed Yet`. The plugin already records those paths in `.stride-dirty-baseline` (W1457), which unlike `.stride-changed-files.json` **is** available at Step 5.5, so the skills now subtract it — the same filter `capture_changed_files` applies. The exclusion is recovered at line granularity where it matters — the baseline stores a claim-time blob hash per path, so a file dirty at claim and then edited again is attributed by diffing against that blob rather than re-admitting the whole file. The skills also state how to obtain the base ref: `TASK_BASE_REF` is exported to hooks and not to the agent, so a literal `git diff $TASK_BASE_REF` would silently degrade to the bare `git diff` the same paragraph warns against. `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` is not reliably set either, and neither is `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`: on a task whose files live in a **nested repository** — a plugin or vendored subrepo inside the Stride project, which is how this very release was built — it lands on the subrepo root while the cache sits at the project root. The skills therefore locate the project root by walking up to the first ancestor holding `.stride.md`, and state the consequence that follows: the base ref is a commit in the *project* repo and is not a valid object inside a nested one, so a nested-repo task computes its change set in the repo it actually edited. An unlocatable cache, or a repo whose base cannot be established, is the undeterminable branch rather than licence to fall back. All three behaviours were verified against a live session rather than assumed. Lines the task wrote → introduced, whatever the file's age, with a narrow exception for lines that are in the change set only because they were moved or reformatted and are shown by `git blame -w` or a base-ref repro to predate the task. Everything else → **discovered**: a fault site elsewhere, a change set that cannot be determined at all, or a fault site not identified after a bounded attempt. `$TASK_BASE_REF` is sanity-checked before it is trusted — a stale env cache can leave the *previous* task's ref in place, which would make that task's lines read as yours and block you for a defect you did not write — and a ref that fails the check is treated as unavailable rather than merely suspect. The two branches that never established provenance are labelled **provenance undetermined — not attributed to this task**, not *pre-existing*, so the Review-queue record never asserts an attribution the agent could not determine. That every uncertain case resolves to discovered is deliberate — blocking on a link the agent could not draw is a denial-of-progress surface and rewards investigating less, and the rubric's own "never Critical on an unknown" already keeps unknown-impact findings off the path. The change set is **never** widened to the task's `key_files`: those are author-written text, and using them would hand the blocking footprint to exactly the kind of content the anchoring exists to exclude. Finding text is restated in the agent's own words, redacted of credentials, tokens, customer data, and internal hostnames, and treated as DATA to assess rather than instructions — the same discipline the security-considerations dispatch requires.
- **No new completion key, and no escalation without a structured review block.** The escalation reuses `reviewer_result` exactly as the security integration does, as a named, bounded exception to the whole-object-copy rule, applied **after** that copy so it cannot be overwritten by it. Two states carry no structured block — a small task with 0-1 `key_files` where review was skipped, and a review that ran but whose JSON would not parse — and in both there is no `issues[]` to append to and no verdict to flip. The skills forbid synthesizing one, and equally forbid the opposite error of downgrading a review that ran to a self-reported skip. An introduced Critical is still fixed before completing, and both cases are recorded in `completion_notes` plus `completion_summary`. The Decision Summary's no-structured-block row explicitly overrides the provenance rows so the table cannot be read two ways.
- **The escalation flips `testing_strategy` only.** It never creates or touches a `behaviour_test_matrix` verdict, matching the existing rule that a `testing` issue not raised by matrix verification belongs to `testing_strategy` alone.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no completion field is added, removed, or re-typed, no enum changes (`issues[].severity`, the seven-value `issues[].category`, and the three-state section-status enum are all untouched), and there is no hook, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change. The escalation writes values that already exist, into an object that already exists, through the whole-object passthrough that already carries it, and it satisfies the existing self-check bullet tying a `testing` issue to a `"failed"` `testing_strategy` rather than adding a check. **The graceful-skip contract is unchanged**: with the plugin absent, no `manual_tests`, or a non-Claude-Code environment, no session runs, there is no finding, and nothing can block completion — exactly as before. Completions emitted against the previous text remain valid.

### Scope

The two orchestrator skills, the completion skill, and the docs. `agents/task-reviewer.md` is deliberately **not** touched: the escalation appends a `category: "testing"` Critical issue and flips `testing_strategy` to `"failed"`, both of which its Consistency rule already covers, so the schema of record needs no new rule to describe it. The `stride-exploratory-testing` plugin is likewise untouched — this task maps its rubric and owns no part of it. The marketplace catalog lives in a separate repo and is synced on its own task, as it was for 1.47.0.

Exploratory sessions run against this change also surfaced four **pre-existing** defects that are not this change's to fix, and — following the policy this release introduces — they were filed rather than folded in: **D201** (the completion self-check's task-supplied-section rule is unsatisfiable for skip-shape and parse-fallback `reviewer_result`), **D202** (five flow artifacts route around Step 5.5 and omit Phase 3.5 entirely, stale since 1.38.0), **D203** (the subagent skill's exploratory gate lacks the plugin-detection rules its own security bullet cites it as the source of), and **D204** (no `workflow_steps` telemetry disposition for the exploratory dispatch, which the security dispatch has had since 1.39.0).

## [1.47.0] - 2026-07-29

### Fixed — the completion gate's bidirectional checkbox is scannable again, by moving text rather than trimming it (D200)

The **MANDATORY pre-submission self-check** is meant to be scanned before every completion, but its bidirectional checkbox had grown to roughly 800 words holding the (a)/(b) rule, the never-delete-downgrade-or-relabel rule, the re-dispatch protocol, three named outcomes with their conditions, a definitional reconciliation against the reviewer's note-only prohibition, and a closing matrix caveat. An agent under time pressure is more likely to skim an 800-word checkbox than run it, which works directly against what a checklist is for.

Every clause in it is load-bearing — each was added to close a specific route to a dropped finding, several after a reviewer found the previous wording insufficient — so this is a **restructure, not a rewrite**. No clause was shortened, paraphrased, reordered, or dropped.

- **`stride-completing-tasks`** — the checkbox keeps the scannable part: the (a)/(b) bidirectional rule, the clarification that the preceding bullet does not license the converse, and the never-delete-downgrade-or-relabel rule. Everything from **"The target state is the section verdict moving to `"failed"`…"** onward — the neutral re-dispatch protocol, the no-hand-merging prohibition, all three outcomes with their conditions, the bare-conclusion-is-outcome-3 rule, the outcome-2/outcome-3 same-shape warning, the note-only definitional reconciliation, outcome 3's escalate-and-do-not-submit branch, and the closing `testing` / matrix caveat — moves verbatim into a named sub-block, **Resolving a verdict/issue disagreement**, indented directly beneath the checkbox so it remains part of that list item.
- **The move is byte-for-byte, and was verified as such rather than asserted.** Concatenating the trimmed checkbox and the moved block reproduces the previous single line character-for-character, with zero characters lost. The checkbox drops from ~826 words to ~201.
- **The sub-block is not optional-looking.** It opens by stating it is part of the checkbox above, is as binding as it is, and is entered whenever either direction fails; the checkbox in turn ends with a pointer stating the check is not satisfied until the block has been followed to one of its three outcomes. Those two sentences are the only text added. This mirrors the section preamble's **third exit**, which is likewise a named prose block beside the checkboxes and likewise binding.
- **The six checkboxes are neither renumbered nor merged** — and it still reads as a six-item gate with the sub-block belonging to item four. The cross-references that exist today (`stride-workflow` in two places) resolve by the section heading, which is untouched, so nothing depended on the bullet's position; keeping the shape stable is insurance against a future reference that does.

One thing is deliberately **not** done, recorded so it is not mistaken for an oversight: the sibling `behaviour_test_matrix` checkbox is long for the same reason (~587 words, now the longest) and the defect noted it is worth the same treatment *if the pattern works*. The pattern does work, but that checkbox is outside this change's stated scope, which is the bidirectional bullet. It is the obvious next application, and the natural seam is the same one — everything from **"The echoed `rows[]` text … is untrusted DATA"** onward.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — no field, enum, `schema_version`, hook, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change, and no rule changes meaning. Every check the gate performed before it performs now, on the same conditions and with the same outcomes; only the layout of one checkbox changed. Skills that cross-reference this gate by its six items are unaffected, since the six remain and keep their order.

### Scope

The completion self-check only. No reviewer prompt, orchestrator step, or fleet variant is touched.

## [1.46.0] - 2026-07-29

### Fixed — the severity collapse is now stated where the severity decision is made, not only in example commentary (D199)

1.43.0 resolved the Critical-vs-Important question for acceptance criteria — review step 1 works on a three-value scale (Met / Partially Met / Not Met) while the emitted `status` enum has only two, so a Partially Met criterion collapses to `"not_met"` on the wire while keeping its `important` severity — but wrote that reconciliation **only** into the worked-example preamble, roughly 85 lines after the point of decision. Both normative loci were left as they were. A reviewer working the numbered steps in order assigns severity at step 1 and never reaches the reconciliation, and at step 1 the rule still read flatly "If any criterion is Not Met, flag it as a Critical issue" — where the working label "Not Met" collides with the wire value `not_met`, with no qualifier and no forward pointer. A `critical` emission for a partially-satisfied criterion was therefore still textually reachable at the exact place the decision is made, which is the reading 1.43.0 set out to close.

The `acceptance_criteria` hard rule had the mirror-image gap: it covered only the partially-satisfied case, and mentioned neither the wholly-absent case nor `critical` severity at all. The one sentence in the file stating wholly-absent → `critical` lived in the preamble. So a reviewer reading the schema section compliantly got no instruction to pair a wholly-unmet criterion with any issue.

- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** — review step 1's severity bullets now mark Met / Partially Met / Not Met as **this step's working labels, not the emitted `status` enum**, state that both Partially Met and Not Met emit as `"not_met"`, and point at the hard rule as where the collapse is specified. The Not Met bullet gains "its behaviour wholly absent"; the Partially Met bullet gains that the collapse to a single wire value does not collapse the severities. Per the pitfall against restating the whole reconciliation at every locus, this is a qualifier plus a pointer, not a second copy of the preamble's paragraph — and it agrees with that paragraph on every clause of the rule.
- **The pairing obligation moves into normative text.** Step 1 gains a bullet, and the hard rule a sentence, requiring every criterion emitted `"not_met"` to be paired with an `issues[]` entry of `category: "acceptance_criteria"` at the matching severity, with the criterion's `evidence` pointing at it rather than dangling. Until now that obligation appeared **only** in worked-example commentary, while `project_checks` — the closest analogue — states its own pairing obligation in both its review step and its schema bullet. The new text mirrors that shape, including the negative half (`"met"` never takes a paired issue), so the two checklists are symmetric.
- **The hard rule covers the wholly-absent case.** It now directs the same `status: "not_met"` (the enum has no third value) with a **`critical`** paired issue, and names the collapse explicitly: three working labels onto two wire values, with severity carrying the distinction the enum cannot.

Two things are deliberately **not** changed, recorded so neither is mistaken for an oversight. The emitted `status` enum stays two-valued — widening it is a wire-shape change and out of scope. And the Consistency rule's matching-category list stays `testing` / `pattern` / `pitfall` / `security`: that rule governs the four `{status, note}` section-verdict objects the Kanban review queue renders as tiles, and `acceptance_criteria` has no such object — its verdicts are per-row `met`/`not_met` inside its own array. Adding it to that parenthetical would change what the rule means rather than extend it; giving it a section verdict would be a wire-shape change. The pairing obligation added above is the symmetric guarantee that does not require either.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no field is added, removed, or re-typed, neither the two-value `acceptance_criteria` `status` enum nor the seven-value `issues[].category` enum changes, and no hook logic, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change is involved. The rules are unchanged in substance; they are stated where the decision is made rather than only in commentary. Reviews emitted against the previous text remain valid — a review that already paired its `not_met` criteria, as the worked example does, satisfies the newly-explicit obligation unchanged.

### Scope

Schema of record only. The other reviewer variants across the fleet mirror `task-reviewer.md` on their next natural sync and are deliberately **not** re-released here — the same scoping 1.42.0 through 1.45.0 applied.

## [1.45.0] - 2026-07-29

### Fixed — the worked example no longer echoes a matrix row `passing` for a behaviour the same review proves defective (D198)

The example's Lifecycle / wiring row claimed the behaviour "The move broadcasts exactly once so every connected board updates", named the test `"broadcasts one move event"`, and echoed `status: "passing"` — while three other legs of the same example state that the fictional `move_task/3` broadcasts **twice**: the third acceptance criterion's `evidence`, the `acceptance_criteria` issue 1.43.0 added, and that issue's `suggested_fix`, which asks the author to "assert the single-emission behaviour in the board LiveView test" — the very test the row declared passing. Under the file's own Mismatch definition a test that genuinely covered "exactly once" could not pass against code that broadcasts twice, so the row was a Mismatch echoed `"passing"`. The example is the only model of what good matrix verification looks like, and it is prefaced "Mimic this shape exactly" — so it was teaching the soft landing the escalation rule exists to forbid. The contradiction predates 1.43.0, which only sharpened it by promoting the finding from an evidence clause to a first-class issue.

**Of the two available fixes, this release takes the second, and the reasoning is recorded here because the choice is not obvious.** Flipping the row to `"failing"` would demonstrate a Mismatch, but the fail-closed escalation rule then requires a matching `category: "testing"` issue to back it, and the example's existing `testing` issue is specifically about the *Concurrency* row's absent test — it would not honestly back a second failing row. That path therefore costs a seventh issue, an `issue_counts.important` bump, a rewrite of the matrix `note`'s "6 of 7 rows verified" claim, and a revision of `testing_strategy.note`, which currently states the broadcast paths *are* covered. Every one of those is a place for a new inconsistency to be introduced into an example whose whole value is internal consistency. The second fix removes the contradiction at its source instead: the row never needed to restate the cardinality, because the cardinality is the third acceptance criterion's claim and its failure is already carried there, by an issue that already exists.

- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** — the Lifecycle / wiring row's `behaviour` becomes "The move broadcasts on the board topic so every connected board updates" and its `test_name` becomes `"broadcasts a move event to connected boards"`. The row now claims only what its named test asserts — that the wiring emits — so it is honestly `"passing"` against a diff that double-emits, and the single-emission requirement remains the third criterion's, still `not_met` and still backed by the `acceptance_criteria` issue. No count moves: `issue_counts` stays 1/4/1 against six `issues[]`, the example `summary` and preamble enumerations are untouched, the matrix `note`'s "6 of 7 rows verified" stays true because the Concurrency row remains the only `"failing"` one, and `testing_strategy.note` stays true because the broadcast path is still covered.
- **The preamble now says why the row is worded that way**, mirroring how it already explains the acceptance-criteria and security legs: a row is honestly `"passing"` only against what its named test actually asserts, a row whose stated `behaviour` the diff contradicts is a Mismatch that must be echoed `"failing"`, and the way to keep those two from disagreeing is to keep a row's `behaviour` to what its test asserts. Without that sentence the same "why doesn't this row fail?" reading returns on the next sweep.
- **A dangling severity reference is corrected while here.** `security_considerations.note` said the bounds-check finding was "raised as the critical security issue above" — but that issue is `important`, and the preamble immediately above it explicitly reserves `critical` for an exploitable finding and calls this one important-not-critical. The note now says "important". This is the same dangling-reference class 1.43.0 fixed on the acceptance-criteria leg, and it was likewise pre-existing at HEAD.

Two gaps are recorded so they are not mistaken for closed. (1) `pattern` remains the one `issues[].category` value with no worked instance — mandated in review step 3, the enum line, the `patterns` verdict and the Consistency rule, demonstrated nowhere; again deliberately not addressed here. (2) Removing this contradiction also removes the file's only near-instance of a **Mismatch**. The escalation cascade is still demonstrated end to end by the Concurrency row — failing row → `category: "testing"` issue → `"failed"` matrix verdict → `"failed"` `testing_strategy` — but that row is the *Missing* flavour (a named test that does not exist), and the Mismatch flavour (a test that exists but does not assert the stated behaviour) now has no worked instance. That is a strictly smaller gap than the one closed here, since the contradiction being removed taught the opposite of the rule, and closing it means a second failing row with its own backing issue — its own change.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no field is added, removed, or re-typed, neither the three-state section-status enum nor the seven-value `issues[].category` enum changes, and no hook logic, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change is involved. Only the example's two synthetic strings and two prose sentences move; the schema-of-record framing is untouched. Reviews emitted against the previous example remain valid.

### Scope

Schema of record only. The other reviewer variants across the fleet mirror `task-reviewer.md` on their next natural sync and are deliberately **not** re-released here — the same scoping 1.42.0, 1.43.0 and 1.44.0 applied. `skills/stride-workflow/SKILL.md` carries the same criterion string in a separate, self-contained worked example that is internally consistent (all criteria `met`, no matrix), so it needs no matching edit.

## [1.44.0] - 2026-07-29

### Fixed — a real finding now outranks `not_assessed`, and the nested-array escalation rules bind in both directions (D193)

Two rules in the schema of record demanded different values for the same reachable state. A reviewer that found a genuine vulnerability on a task supplying no `security_considerations` was told by the empty-section rule to emit `not_assessed`, and by the Consistency rule to emit `failed` — because the finding requires a `category: "security"` issue and any such issue flips its section. There was no documented winner, and the cheapest way out of the conflict was to make the finding go away. That is the failure mode this release exists to prevent, not a resolution to it.

The collision is **not** security-specific. The `testing` instance is already live in the file: a task supplying a `behaviour_test_matrix` but no `testing_strategy` gets a `failing` row, which raises a `category: "testing"` issue, which the Consistency rule explicitly flips `testing_strategy` on — against a `not_assessed` the empty-section rule calls mandatory. The credential carve-out added in 1.41.0 was the *security* instance of the same cross-section flip; its `testing`, `pitfall` and `pattern` siblings were undocumented and had no carve-out at all.

- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** — the four-tile verdict rule gains a precedence clause: `not_assessed` means *there was nothing to assess*, so it cannot survive a finding actually made. An empty task section plus a genuine matching-category issue is `"failed"`. `not_assessed` remains correct for an empty section that produced no finding. **The rule for a task-supplied section is untouched** — it is still always `passed`/`failed` — and the two cases are stated as disjoint rather than as an exception, so nothing here reopens D60.
- **Category follows the finding, not the section's population.** Which of the seven `issues[].category` values applies is decided by which numbered review step the finding belongs to, never by whether the task filled in the matching section. The rule binds downward — dropping a finding, filing a step-5 security finding as `code_quality`, downgrading it to `minor` to duck the section flip, or confining it to prose or a section `note` are each named as a **worse defect than the verdict conflict they avoid** — and upward, so an ordinary `code_quality` observation is not relabelled into an empty section to make it look assessed.
- **Review step 5 no longer scopes the security review to the list.** Every clause was conditioned on "If `security_considerations` is provided", so on a task that listed nothing the reviewer had no instruction to assess security at all and the precedence rule above would have been dormant. The listed considerations now say which implications the author wants *confirmed*; they do not bound what is *reviewed*. The dimensions step 5 already names are checked on every diff, within the diff's own scope and at the same Important-unless-exploitable severity split.
- **The credential carve-out is reframed as the worked instance of the general rule, not a separate exception.** Its trigger — a credential in the task's own matrix row — is unchanged and unwidened; it is simply no longer the only case, and every parenthetical that called it "the one narrow exception" — including the one inside review step 4 itself, which a reader meets *before* the general rule — now points at the general rule so none can be read as closing the set.
- **The nested `considerations[]` escalation rule binds in both directions — and the reverse direction is legitimate, not forbidden.** A `"failed"` section beside an all-`mitigated` array is the correct output when the failure originates outside the task's listed considerations. The governing reason is what the array is: `considerations[]` breaks down *one* of the verdict's inputs — the task's listed items — while the verdict draws on the whole step-5 review, so a finding outside the list has no array slot to live in. Forbidding the state would force either suppressing that finding or dishonestly relabelling a listed item `partial`; both are worse. The v1.41.0 credential carve-out can reach the same state on a task that did supply considerations, which is why a flat prohibition would also have cut across an already-documented path. Two obligations bound it: the `"failed"` verdict is still backed by a matching `category: "security"` issue, and any listed consideration the failure *does* touch is echoed `partial`/`unmitigated` rather than `mitigated`. The rule says explicitly that the mismatch is never to be "fixed" by flipping the section to `passed` or by trimming the issue.
- **The `behaviour_test_matrix` rows rule gets the opposite reverse direction, and the asymmetry is stated rather than papered over.** `rows[]` is the *complete* enumeration of everything the matrix verdict draws on — rows are echoed from the task's matrix and never invented, and every route to `"failed"` (Missing, Mismatch, a waiver no longer true, a credential-bearing row) lands on a `"failing"` row. So a `"failed"` matrix verdict with no `"failing"` row **is** a defect, and that rule no longer claims to mirror the `considerations` rule in this direction. The fix is to correct the row's judgement, never to invent a row; a `testing` issue genuinely not about any row belongs to `testing_strategy` alone. One honest exit is named so the only remedy is not the fabrication-adjacent one: if no row is Missing or Mismatch, the **verdict** was wrong rather than the rows — it should be `"passed"`, and the `testing` issue that prompted `"failed"` stands and still flips `testing_strategy`. The finding survives; only the tile moves.
- **Two further routes to silence are closed on the reviewer side.** The file's terminal *Important constraints* block still said "Do not flag issues that are outside the scope of the current task" — the last line a reviewer reads, and on a task that declared no `security_considerations` it read as permission to do exactly what the new step-5 clause forbids. It now carries an exception for a security finding in the diff, with the maxim bound to that exception so the prohibition stays operative for every other category, and "declining to raise it on task-scope grounds" is named as a fifth escape alongside the original four. Review step 5 also now covers the **credible-but-unconfirmable** finding: when exploitability cannot be confirmed without reading code the diff does not contain, the finding is raised at `important` with the uncertainty stated and the unread location named, rather than withheld — naming a location is not exploring it, so the "only review the diff provided" constraint is untouched.
- **`stride-completing-tasks`** gains one self-check bullet and amends two. The new bullet gates both directions of section-verdict/issue agreement, including the previously ungated case of a matching-category issue beside a `not_assessed` empty section — **or an omitted verdict object**, which was cheaper than `not_assessed` and previously unguarded — and states that the existing no-`not_assessed`-for-a-task-supplied-section bullet does not license the converse. **Its terminal branch is fail-closed and can end in escalation rather than submission.** When the gate fails, the agent re-dispatches the reviewer with the finding named neutrally, and the re-run has three outcomes: it raises the finding (take that review); it rejects the finding **with a stated mechanism** — a `file:line`, a guard, a caller contract — placed in the section `note` and recorded in `completion_notes` and `completion_summary` (a corrected review, take it); or it neither raises nor explains, in which case the agent **must not submit** and escalates to the human in-session with the task left claimed. Outcomes 2 and 3 ship the same `reviewer_result` shape, so the stated mechanism is the only discriminator and doubt resolves toward the block. Hand-merging two reviews is forbidden outright — it would assemble a `reviewer_result` no single review emitted. **This is the first branch of the completion gate that does not end in a submission.** The matrix bullet gains the `"failed"`-requires-a-`"failing"`-row converse; the nested-considerations bullet gains the note that the legitimate all-`mitigated`-beside-`failed` state is not a gap to close. The "no deep review ran → array absent → never required" clause is unchanged.

Two adjacent gaps are deliberately **not** closed here, recorded so they are not mistaken for closed. (1) The worked example still demonstrates only the forward escalation — a `failed` section with an `unmitigated` entry. The newly-legitimate `failed`-beside-all-`mitigated` state has no worked instance, and by this file's own stated theory (1.42.0, 1.43.0: models copy examples more reliably than prose) that predicts reviewers will "correct" it. Closing it means a second full example, which is its own change. (2) `stride-workflow`'s Step 5 merge block hard-codes `severity: "critical"` for every escalated consideration, against review step 5's Important-unless-exploitable split; that divergence predates this release and is unrelated to the verdict conflict.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no field is added, removed, or re-typed, neither the three-state section-status enum nor the seven-value `issues[].category` enum changes, and no hook logic, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change is involved. The one state these rules newly *bless* — a `failed` `security_considerations` beside an all-`mitigated` `considerations[]` — was already correct output and is now documented as such. The one state they newly *forbid* — a `failed` `behaviour_test_matrix` with no `failing` row — **was reachable before this release**, via the Consistency rule's broad reading (any `testing` issue flips the matrix) or the unmapped legitimately-`planned`-row case, so a stored review may carry that shape. Nothing re-validates stored `reviewer_result` rows, so this is a forward constraint rather than a retroactive rejection; the Consistency rule's scoping is now stated explicitly and the honest exit for the `planned`-row case is named, so the shape should stop being produced.

### Scope

Schema of record plus the completion self-check that gates it. The other reviewer variants across the fleet mirror `task-reviewer.md` on their next natural sync and are deliberately **not** re-released here — the same scoping 1.42.0 and 1.43.0 applied. `stride-workflow`'s Step 5 merge block is likewise out of scope: it escalates upward only, makes no reverse-direction claim, and already forbids downgrading to `passed` on anomalies, so it cannot produce either state these rules forbid. Two further items are deliberately deferred and recorded so they are not mistaken for closed: (a) `stride-workflow` and `stride-subagent-workflow` each summarise the completion gate as three checks ending "no `not_assessed` for a task-supplied section" — now an under-count of a six-bullet gate, and the very framing whose converse this release adds; and (b) the new "any listed consideration the failure *does* touch must be `partial`/`unmitigated`" obligation is a semantic judgement, while the gate compares "counts, keys, and status enums only", so it is stated with a checkable proxy rather than fully enforced. Its gate on non-empty `security_considerations` is unaffected — it gates the *specialist* deep dispatch, while review step 5's dimension checks are the *generalist* reviewer's and now run regardless.

## [1.43.0] - 2026-07-29

### Fixed — the worked example's `not_met` acceptance criterion now has a backing issue, and its dangling reference resolves (D191)

The worked example's third acceptance criterion ("PubSub broadcast emitted exactly once per move") carried `status: "not_met"` with an `evidence` string ending "…see the critical issue above" — but no such issue existed. The example's `issues[]` carried `pitfall`, `security`, `project_check`, `testing` and `code_quality`, none of them about broadcasting. So the cross-reference dangled, and the example demonstrated the opposite of what two rules in the same file require: review step 1 ("If any criterion is Not Met, flag it as a Critical issue") and the `acceptance_criteria` hard rule ("If a criterion is partially satisfied, set `status: "not_met"`, describe the gap in `evidence`, and add a corresponding `important` entry to `issues`"). This is the same argument 1.42.0 (W1941) made for `security`: models copy examples far more reliably than they follow prose, so a category that is mandated but never demonstrated is systematically under-emitted. That leaves `pattern` as the one remaining undemonstrated category — mandated in review step 3, the enum line, the `patterns` verdict and the Consistency rule, and demonstrated in no worked instance. It is deliberately **not** addressed here: closing it means adding a seventh issue and flipping the example's `patterns` verdict to `failed`, which is its own change rather than a rider on this one. Recorded so the gap is not mistaken for closed.

- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** — the worked example gains an `issues[]` entry with `category: "acceptance_criteria"` describing the synthetic double broadcast in the example's own fictional `move_task/3`, placed among the important entries directly after the critical pitfall. `issue_counts.important` goes 3 → 4, the example `summary` gains the matching clause, and the third criterion's `evidence` now points at that entry instead of at a critical issue that never existed.
- **The preamble's two enumerations now agree.** It previously listed the five issues and then appended "and a not-met acceptance criterion" as a sixth item *outside* that enumeration — the framing that made the criterion a peer of the issue list rather than something backed by it. The criterion is now folded in as one of the six issues, and the preamble gains prose describing how the acceptance-criteria legs relate, mirroring the treatment the security legs already had.
- **The Critical-vs-Important split is resolved in the text rather than left to reflex.** Review step 1 works on a three-value scale (Met / Partially Met / Not Met) and assigns Critical to Not Met and Important to Partially Met, while the emitted `status` enum has only two values — so a Partially Met criterion collapses to `"not_met"` on the wire while keeping `important` severity, which is exactly what the hard rule directs. The two rules were never in conflict; only the collapse was undocumented. The preamble now says so, states that this example is the partially-satisfied case (the broadcast is emitted, just twice), and reserves `critical` for a criterion whose behaviour is wholly absent — so the single worked instance cannot be copied into systematic severity inflation.

The seven-value `issues[].category` enum line is unchanged — `"acceptance_criteria"` was already legal there; only a worked instance of it was missing. The finding is synthetic, mirroring the example's existing fictional `lib/kanban/tasks.ex` subject.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no field is added, removed, or re-typed, and no hook logic, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change is involved. Reviews emitted against the previous example remain valid.

### Scope

Schema of record only. The other reviewer variants across the fleet mirror this file on their next natural sync and are deliberately **not** re-released here — a fleet-wide cascade for an example addition is not warranted, the same scoping 1.42.0 applied.

## [1.42.0] - 2026-07-28

### Added — a worked `category: "security"` example in the reviewer schema of record (W1941)

Every reviewer prompt mandates `category: "project_check"` in prose **and** demonstrates it in the worked JSON example, whereas `category: "security"` was mandated in four separate places and demonstrated nowhere in the tree. Models copy examples far more reliably than they follow prose, which predicts security issues are systematically under-emitted — leaving the fail-closed escalation rules built in 1.39.0 (G381/G382) unsatisfied in practice, since each requires a `category: "security"` issue to back a `failed` `security_considerations` verdict.

- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** — the worked example now carries a fifth `issues[]` entry with `category: "security"`, describing a synthetic unaddressed bounds-check on the example's own fictional `move_task/3`. The example's `security_considerations` moves with it: the section `status` flips `passed` → `failed`, its second `considerations[]` entry flips `mitigated` → `unmitigated`, and the section `note` explains which of the two listed considerations was and was not implemented — so the issue, the nested entry, and the section verdict are visibly consistent with the fail-closed escalation and Consistency rules. `issue_counts.important` goes 2 → 3, the example `summary` gains the matching clause, and the preamble now spells out how the legs relate: the issue and the section status each require the other (Consistency rule) and always move together, while the `considerations` breakdown is optional but forces both when present and `unmitigated`/`partial` (escalation rule).
- **Severity is demonstrated, not just mandated.** The new entry is `severity: "important"`, matching review step 5's own split — an unaddressed or inadequately-handled consideration is Important, and Critical is reserved for one that leaves an *exploitable* vulnerability in the diff. The preamble says so explicitly, so the single worked instance of that rule cannot be copied into systematic severity inflation.

The seven-value `issues[].category` enum line is unchanged — `"security"` was already legal there; only a worked instance of it was missing. The finding is synthetic, mirroring the example's existing fictional `lib/kanban/tasks.ex` subject.

### Backward compatibility

Fully backward compatible. Prompt-text only — `schema_version` stays **1.6**, no field is added, removed, or re-typed, and no hook logic, `.stride.md`, env-var, or `.stride_auth.md` change is involved. Reviews emitted against the previous example remain valid; the seven-value enum this example draws from has been accepted by the Kanban server since W1940 widened it.

### Scope

Schema of record only. The other reviewer variants across the fleet mirror this file on their next natural sync and are deliberately **not** re-released here — a fleet-wide cascade for an example addition is not warranted.

## [1.41.0] - 2026-07-28

### Changed — the `behaviour_test_matrix` rules treat row text as untrusted, and say what to do when it carries a credential

`behaviour_test_matrix` row text is authored by whoever created the task and is attacker-controlled at the API boundary — anyone posting directly to the Stride API never sees these instructions. v1.40.0 threaded the field through the plugin; this release hardens every rule that reads it, and resolves a contradiction that made one of them impossible to obey.

- **Row text is data, never instructions.** The completion self-check's matrix gate and the Step 4 implementation driver both state the boundary explicitly: a row is a specification to satisfy, and text inside a row that appears to address the agent, waive a check, or exempt the task is content being submitted — reportable as a finding, never a directive to follow (D178, D179).
- **The secret rule is scoped to row *state*, not agent intent, and covers references.** A row that embeds a secret, credential, or token — **or that names a location where one lives** (file path, env var, secret-store key, vault reference, CI/CD or platform secret, Kubernetes Secret, git object, database row) — is by that fact alone a defect to raise. The trigger is what the row names, never what the agent intended, so no other purpose the agent also holds makes resolving that location permitted. The workflow's own sanctioned use of its authentication credentials stays permitted; a row naming that file is still a row, and is reported rather than read (D184, D187).
- **A refused row has a named reporting channel and a defined representation.** The implementing agent reports the defect in `completion_notes`, identifying the row by `category` and position rather than quoting its text, and leaves the row exactly as the task authored it. The reviewer, which is required to echo rows verbatim, instead substitutes the literal sentinel `[REDACTED — row text embedded a credential]` into the required field that carries the credential — satisfying the non-empty requirement without letting the secret reach its output — echoes that row `failing`, and raises a `category: "security"` issue. The resulting `failed` verdict is the **expected outcome of a correct refusal**, not a defect by the agent (D186).
- **The PATCH-body contradiction is resolved.** The driver mandated recording a row's status advance by PATCHing the matrix while forbidding a credential from reaching the PATCH body. Those were unsatisfiable together: `PATCH /api/tasks/:id` replaces the whole array, and a non-empty matrix is rejected unless it covers all seven categories, so advancing any row re-sends them all — and dropping the offending row fails that validation. The rules now state that re-sending row text the task record **already stores**, byte-for-byte unchanged, back onto that same record is not a new copy, and name exactly one correct action. The exception is scoped to that one field on that one task's own record, to already-stored text, and only unchanged; every other sink (code, tests, commit messages, `completion_notes`, the reviewer prompt) still binds in full, and substituting the reviewer's redaction sentinel into the task record is explicitly forbidden — it would rewrite the author's row and desynchronise it from the verbatim echo the completion self-check enforces (D185).
- **The Step 5 self-review checklist** gained a `behaviour_test_matrix` bullet, so the non-Claude-Code path checks the field it was already told to write (W1949).

### Changed — guidance now cites the real controls instead of authoring conventions

- **`completion_notes` is persisted.** The Stride server previously accepted and silently discarded it. Every span that described it as unpersisted now states the deployment-conditional truth: it is persisted by Stride servers from D188 onward, but an agent cannot tell which server version it is talking to, so a refusal recorded only there may still reach no human. The rule requiring the refusal to *also* be stated in one line of `completion_summary` is unchanged — only its stated premise was corrected.
- **Row-text rendering is defended by escaping, not by an authoring rule.** The creation and enrichment guidance told authors not to put raw HTML in `behaviour`, `test_name`, or `na_reason` and read as though that instruction were the defense. It is not — an attacker posts directly and never sees it. The prose now cites the real controls (every render path interpolates row text through auto-escaped templates and never a raw-HTML helper; the API hard-rejects an out-of-vocabulary `category` or `status`), keeps the no-raw-HTML rule as hygiene, and separates out the secrets rule as genuinely authoring-only — nothing on the server strips a credential from row text (W1947).

## [1.40.0] - 2026-07-26

### Added — `behaviour_test_matrix` threaded through populate, verify, and utilize (G381)

The Kanban app stores a task's **behaviour/test matrix** — one row per behaviour paired with the test that covers it, across seven fixed categories. This release makes the plugin actually *use* it: document it as a creation field, populate it during enrichment, verify it during review, and drive implementation from it.

The field is **OPTIONAL throughout**. It is deliberately **not** added to the five review_queue-scored fields (`acceptance_criteria`, `testing_strategy`, `security_considerations`, `pitfalls`, `patterns_to_follow`), so a task without a matrix is never penalized and never renders an empty pill.

- **`stride-creating-tasks`** documents `behaviour_test_matrix` as a structured creation field: a new **Embedded Object Formats** subsection (four WRONG cases that each fail for a real schema reason, plus a labelled RIGHT excerpt), a Field Quick Reference row, a Recommended-fields checklist entry, and a full seven-row sample in the Complete Task Object example. Row contract: `category` (one of the 7 fixed categories), `behaviour`, and `status` are required; `test_name` is required unless the row is waived (`status: "not_applicable"` or an N/A-like `test_name`), in which case `na_reason` is required instead; `type` is `unit`/`integration`/`manual` or a `/`-joined combination; `position` is order-bearing. **All-or-nothing:** an absent or empty matrix passes, but a non-empty one must carry a row for every one of the seven categories.
- **`stride-creating-goals`** documents the identical shape for nested tasks — no batch-specific variation.
- **`stride-enriching-tasks`** builds the matrix in Phase 2 Step 3, projecting the `unit_tests` / `integration_tests` / `manual_tests` / `edge_cases` it just derived onto the seven categories, and adds it to the pre-submission checklist (now **18 items**).
- **`stride-agents/task-enricher`** populates it on the Claude Code path — the only path a real enrichment run reads. Emission is the default whenever the testing analysis produced test cases; omission is reserved for a task with genuinely no testable behaviour. Rows authored at enrichment time are `status: "planned"` unless honestly waived as `"not_applicable"`, and each names a real test or carries an `na_reason`, and never record secrets or raw HTML. Its checklist is likewise now **18 items**, with `behaviour_test_matrix` as the one item that may legitimately be omitted.
- **`stride-agents/task-reviewer`** schema bumps to `schema_version` **1.6**: a **Behaviour/Test Matrix Verification** pass in review step 4 locates the test each row names and judges the row *Verified* / *Missing* / *Mismatch*, plus a new **OPTIONAL** top-level `behaviour_test_matrix` verdict object `{ status, note, rows[] }` modeled on the `security_considerations.considerations[]` precedent.
